Page 13

| Mahabharata Adiparva | Anuśasanaparva Aśvāsya 001 Salutations to Narayana and to the man, the hero of men! To the gods and the sages, thus let victory be proclaimed! 000 | The son of a charioteer, Lohamṛga, does not hesitate to speak of the ancient tales of the Paurāṇikas. 001 | Seated in the assembly, the twenty-one sages, When will the earth be free from the burden of the sinful? 002 | Those who do not hesitate to speak of the stories of the great sages, Let them be honored with the tales of the past. 003 | Thus, the sages, having performed their penance, With their minds focused, they attained the highest state. 004 | Then, those who are devoted to the sages, Having taken their seats, they began to speak with humility. 005 | 1

Page 14

2 Anumani Pava Sukhāsīnaṁ tataṁ tu viśvāmitra upalaṁ ca | Atha pṛcchatiṣṭhā kiṁ kṛtāṁ vāyathāḥ | 006 | Kuta āgacchati saute chāyaṁ viśvitrayā | Kālaḥ kamalapadmaḥ śaṁsati mām | 007 | Sūta uvāca | Janamejayaḥ rājāḥ sapta saṁsāre mahābhūtaḥ | Samīpe pāṭhī veśyaṁ saṁkṣāriṇi ca | 008 | Kṛtāḥ pāyanoḥ sukhāḥ vividhāḥ kathāḥ | Kithatāpi vidhavā vaiśaṁpāyaneṇa vai | 009 | Sūryāṁhaṁ tāṁ vicitrāḥ mahābhāratasaṁhitāḥ | Bṛhīn saṁpirāṇī tīthāḥ saṁyatanāni ca | 010 | Samāpakam nāma puṣyaṁ vijinṣevitam | Gatavāni taṁ deśaṁ yūṁ yathābhavatu ra | 011 | Pāṇḍavānāṁ kuṭṭanāṁ ca sarveṣāṁ ca mahīṭatām | 011 | Idṛṣṭurāgatāṁ bhavatāṁ iha | Āyuṣmān sarveva bhūtā hi me matāḥ | 012 | Aśvamedhe mahābhāgāḥ sūryaḥ pāvaka vachaḥ | Kṛtābhiṣekāḥ śucayaḥ kṛtajñāḥ pratāhyaḥ | 013 | Bhavata āstate kīmahaṁ vijñāḥ | 013 |

Page 15

"001 3 The stories of the Puranas or the Dharma-sutras, Thus spoke the great sages of mankind. 014 The sages said: By the sage Vaipāyana, the Purana is declared supreme; It is adorned with the essence of the divine, worthy of worship. 015 With the knowledge of the Vedas, The essence of the teachings is established. 016 In the history of Bharat, the Puranas are connected With various narratives, enriched with diverse stories. 017 In the assembly of King Janamejaya, Vaishampayana spoke, As the sage recited, the teachings of Vaipāyana were revealed. 018 The Vedas, with their profound meanings, Are united in the essence of the sacred texts, dispelling the fear of sin. 019 Soot said: I bow to the supreme Ishana, the best of the best, The eternal truth, the one and only. 020 Thus, it is said that the supreme Purana is the ultimate refuge For those who seek liberation from worldly attachments. 021 I offer my respects to the sage, The one who pervades all beings, the essence of the universe. 022 May purity and virtue prevail."

Page 16

4 Anuśarman The great sage, worshipped in all worlds, is the great one | The wise one, whose mind is absorbed in the light of knowledge. | 023 | The poets, some of whom are skilled in the art of poetry, | Speak of the ancient history of this world. | 024 | This, however, is the great one in this world, | Whose essence is described by the wise as the source of all. | 025 | With auspicious times, the good ones are present, | Various forms of life are established in different ways. | 026 | In the world of the unmanifest, where all is covered, | The great seed of the wise has become one. | 027 | In the beginning of the ages, the creation is spoken of, | That which is eternal is called the essence of all. | 028 | The wondrous and the beautiful have all attained equality, | The cause of all is the eternal truth. | 029 | Where the great one is established, who is the supreme being, | Who is the lord of the universe, who is the ultimate truth? | 030 | The wise ones, who are steadfast in their resolve, | Thus, the lords of the wise are born from the lineage of the great. | 031 | The one who is the essence of all, whom all sages praise, | The deities, the Vasus, and the others are also present. | 032 | Wherever there are the great ones, the wise ones are present.

Page 17

1. Thus, the seven sages, pure in their actions, are born. 2. The kings, many and all, are endowed with qualities. 3. Water, earth, air, and the directions are established. 4. The months and fortnights are cyclic; from them, the world is born. 5. Whatever is created, whether mobile or immobile, again becomes manifest in the cycle of ages. 6. As the seasons change, various forms of nourishment arise; thus, they are the essence of the ages. 7. Thus, this is the cause of the destruction of beings; in the world, it is eternally present. 8. The elements of creation and destruction are also present; the essence of the gods is the source of creation. 9. The great beings, endowed with strength, are the manifestations of the sun; they are the source of light. 10. The moon, shining brightly, is the source of all beings; the divine beings are nourished by it. 11. The sun, the moon, and the stars are the sources of light; the ten directions are illuminated by them. 12. Ten suns, shining together, create a hundredfold brightness; thus, the ten qualities are manifested. 13. From ten suns, tenfold brightness arises; then, the ten qualities are manifested in a hundredfold manner.

Page 18

6 Anumani Pava Moreover, ten qualities are well-known. This is the lineage of the Bharatas. 044 Yayati, the lineage of kings, is indeed the source of many lineages, born of the past. 045 All beings are subject to the laws of nature, and the Vedic knowledge is the essence of righteousness and desire. 046 Righteousness and desire are variously defined, and the rules of the world are established by sages. 047 The histories are diverse and even contradictory. Here, all minds are engaged in the pursuit of truth. 048 The sages of the past have established the principles of the world. Here, the essence of existence is maintained. 049 In Bharat, some are engaged in various activities, while others are similarly occupied. 050 Various teachings illuminate the minds of the wise. Let those who are skilled uphold the truth. 051 Through penance, the eternal Veda is attained, and this history is indeed a source of strength. 052 Parashara, the sage, is known for his wisdom, and through the grace of the mother, the wise attain knowledge. 053

Page 19

1. "Aṅgāya 001 7 In the assembly of the wise, the sage Vaśiṣṭha spoke. The Kauravas, having gathered, were engaged in discussion. 054 2. Dhritarāṣhṭra and Pāṇḍu went to the sage Dīrghatapas, who was meditating. 055 3. Indeed, it is the truth. 4. Among those born, those who have grown, and those who have departed, the supreme song is sung. In the world, men are not without their deeds. 056 5. Janamejaya asked the sage, "Who is the one seated here?" He ruled over the assembly, the sage Vaiśaṁpāyana. 057 6. He, along with the sages, began to narrate the Bharata. The stories of the past were recounted again and again. 058 7. The sage, with a pure lineage, was devoted to righteousness. Vaśiṣṭha, the sage, was not without strength. 059 8. The great sage Vāsudeva, of the Pāṇḍavas, was the one who spoke. He was the one who had the knowledge of the scriptures. 060 9. In the twenty-fourth chapter, the Bharata is presented. The wise ones, with their knowledge, speak of it. 061 10. Then, the sage, having completed a hundred, performed the great sacrifice. He was the one who had the knowledge of the Vedas. 062 11. This is the sage Vaśiṣṭha, the one who is the first among the sages. Then, having completed the sacrifice, he spoke the truth. 063 12. Nārada, the sage, was not without the divine. He was the father of the sages."

Page 20

8 Anumani-pavṛha In the assembly of the cows, the parrot spoke. 064 The wealth of the wise is great; the fruit of the tree is the king, Dhritarashtra, who is thoughtful. 065 In the battle, the great one is righteous; the branches are Bhimasena's. The fruit of the tree is the king, Krishna, and the one who is wise. 066 The learned one, the great sage, is skilled in hunting; he is a friend of the deer. 067 The deer, when caught, is a source of danger; the path of the wise is filled with various teachings. 068 The sage, along with the mother, is surrounded by great ones; they are the great ones of the forest and the great ones of the rivers. 070 The sages, at that time, were endowed with knowledge; they were like the shining sun, illuminating the world. 071 The wise ones, with their knowledge, are like the sun; the learned ones are like the moon, illuminating the night. 072 Those who are knowledgeable are like the wise; the learned ones are like the great sages, who are free from desires. 073 There are some who say, "This is it," and when the learned one dies, how do they say, "This is it"? 074

Page 21

1. The arrival is fully established in the assembly of the learned. 2. The directions are filled with sounds, and the essence of the beings is rooted. 3. Auspicious deeds are performed, and the essence of the offerings is manifested. 4. The joy of all the townspeople is evident; the divine presence is established. 5. The complete offerings are made with various items, and the worship is done without hesitation. 6. With purity and cleanliness, they became calm; Bhimasena held the fort with strength. 7. The world was pleased with the beauty of the young maidens and their graceful conduct. 8. Then, the king, with great strength, performed the royal sacrifice. 9. Thus, in the world, the wealth of all beings was established; like the sun, the glory in battle arose. 10. The king, with his great assembly, performed the grand Rajasuya sacrifice.

Page 22

10 Anuśarman In the assembly of the great Rajasuya, all the warriors are gathered together with their qualities. By the prowess of Bhima, the son of Kunti, Jarasandha and others were slain. With great strength, The wealth of the earth, the treasures, and the riches of the cows were brought forth. Thus, the great assembly of the Pandavas was established, and the glory of the great king was proclaimed. The assembly was adorned by the presence of the divine, and the Pandavas were honored in that great gathering. As the sun rises, the great Bhima shines forth, and the assembly is filled with joy. The various enjoyments and pleasures were distributed, and Dhritarashtra was filled with sorrow. The wrath of Dhritarashtra arose due to the great anger of the Pandavas. The minds of the Kauravas were filled with anxiety, and they were troubled by the news of the Pandavas' victory. The great Bhima, the son of Kunti, was fierce and powerful, and he was a source of fear for the Kauravas. Victory to the Pandavas, the great warriors!

12 Anuṣṭup When the auspicious moon rises in the sky; adorned with the beautiful Mādhavī flower, The valiant heroes will depart; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 103 When the auspicious moon rises for the king of gods; with arrows and the chariot, And the great bow is drawn; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 104 When the auspicious moon rises in the battlefield; with the Parājita flower by the Soubala, And the uninvited guests are driven away; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 105 When the auspicious moon rises with the Pāṇḍava; the assembly is well-governed and united, The lord of the earth is without a lord; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 106 When the auspicious moon rises with various signs; in the forest of the righteous, The great ones will be honored by the wise; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 107 When the auspicious moon rises with the great ones; the righteous king arrives in the forest, The great ones will be honored by the wise; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 108 When the auspicious moon rises for the god of gods; the great one is worshipped, When the auspicious moon rises; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 109 When the auspicious moon rises for the wealthy; as the righteous are properly honored, The learned will be respected; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 110 When the auspicious moon rises with the great assembly; the mighty Bhīma is present, In the presence of men of great honor; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 111 When the auspicious moon rises with the sound of drums; the great gathering of the cows and the herds, Of those who are devoted to their children; then victory will be assured in the destruction. 112 When the auspicious moon rises with the righteous; the righteous king is present with the charioteer.

"Invocation 001 13 With the sound of the conch and the drum, victory is assured; for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 113 When the army of my kin is assembled; with the chariot of the great warrior, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 114 When the army is led by the great charioteer; with the auspicious banner, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 115 When the army is led by the noble ones; with the auspicious weapons, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 116 When the army is led by the divine Narayana; I, the lord of the world, am present; then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 117 Thus spoke Narada. When the army is led by Madhava, the Lord of Vāsudeva; with the great warriors assembled, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 118 When the army is led by the one with the conch and the bow; with the great charioteer, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 119 When the army is led by Vāsudeva, the Lord of the chariot; with the chariots of the warriors assembled, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 120 When the army is led by the Lord of Vāsudeva; and also by Bhishma and Shantanu, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 121 When the army is led by the conch, Bhishma speaks; I am not one to be defeated in battle; thus, for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 122 When the army is led by the divine Vāsudeva; with the auspicious gathering, then for the destruction of the enemy, victory is certain. 123 Thus, the wealth of the great warriors is assured."

14 Anumani Pava When the charioteer, seated on the chariot, is afflicted by the lotus of the herb; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 124 Again, When the charioteer, terrified by the fierce enemy; is surrounded by the chariots of the enemy; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 125 When the charioteer, struck down in battle; is defeated by the arrows of the enemy; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 126 When the charioteer, lying in the autumn; is a hero, steadfast in battle; when the enemy is terrified by the remaining warriors; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 127 When the charioteer, lying in the peaceful; is questioned by the water; when the earth is shaken by the terrified enemy; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 128 Again, When the charioteer, with a calm mind, is victorious; in the battle of the Kauravas; when the enemy is defeated by the words of the wise; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 129 When the charioteer, in the midst of the battle; is struck by the arrows of the enemy; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 130 When the charioteer, with a clear mind, is victorious; in the battle of the wise; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 131 Again, When the charioteer, with a strong heart; is surrounded by the enemy; when the hero is alone, steadfast in battle; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 132 When the charioteer, with a fierce heart; is surrounded by the enemy; when the great warriors are defeated; then, in the body of the victorious, there is no destruction. 133

1. When the medicinal herb is prepared, then the victory is assured; thus, there is no destruction. 2. With the help of the wise, peace is attained. 3. When the medicinal herb is prepared with the essence of the earth, the enemies are vanquished. 4. The sage, having prepared the medicinal herb, curses with the curse of the other. 5. The beautiful flower, devoid of fragrance; thus, the father is bereft of sons. 6. What has been done by the wise is the essence of the body; the king is adorned with the essence of the earth. 7. How many remain, my ten; the wise are not without sorrow. 8. The one who is afflicted by the darkness of ignorance does not perceive; my mind is indeed troubled. 9. 18 Anumani (a type of gem) The earth, knowing the truth, is filled with various qualities. From the glory of the scriptures, those who are subject to time have passed away. Victory to the great charioteer, the hero, the best among the victorious! Also to the auspicious Ridhadeva and the great physician! The conqueror of the demon, the one who is victorious in the bed of death, Mighty and powerful, the one who is the best among the strong! Rama, the son of Dasharatha, and Bhagiratha, And also Bharata. By the auspicious deeds of Yayati, the one who is worshipped by the gods, The earth is made prosperous by the offerings of the great sages. Thus, with the fourteenth chapter, the great sage Surishi Has sung the glories of the ancient kings who were powerful and virtuous. The kings of the past, who were strong and valiant, The great charioteers, all gathered together with their qualities. The one who is a great warrior, who is fearless and has great strength, With the young and brave Kaku, the one who is known as Raghu. The victorious, the one who has conquered fear, the great one, Ushinar, the one with the bow, the one who is known as Shuma. The one who is the son of the ocean, Sagara, the one who is invincible, The one with the axe, the one who is the protector, the one who is sinless. The gods are pleased with the one who is the best, the one who is the greatest.

20 Anumani-pavṛha Thus, the one who is not to be grieved is not to be grieved by the divine, due to the special nature of fate. 186 The one who is bound by the creator does not become liberated. The root of time is the existence and non-existence of all beings, which is happiness and sorrow. 187 Time cooks the beings; time gathers the created. The one who is bound by time, time again calms. 188 Time differentiates between auspicious and inauspicious states. Time, the lord of all, again releases the created. 189 Time is equal to all beings, maintaining their movement. 189 The states of the past, present, and future are not to be grasped by the one who is bound by time. 190 Soot spoke: Even though the Upanishads are pure, they are not to be disregarded. The Bharatiya tradition is to be honored in the footnotes. 191 In the purest of the pure, all sins are completely removed. 191 The divine beings, the great sages, and the kings of the earth, who are engaged in auspicious deeds, are indeed the great serpents. 192 The blessed one, the eternal god, is indeed the one who grants eternal life and pure liberation. 193

**Pauṇḍavā** 49 **Pauṇḍavā** **Ānāya 003** Sūta said: Janamejaya, surrounded by his brothers, approached the sage. The brothers were Ṛṣabha, Ṛṣabha, and Bhīma. 001. They were seated in meditation, and Janamejaya, distressed by the loss of his mother, went to her. 002. The mother, seeing him distressed, spoke: "Why are you crying? Who has caused you pain?" 003. He, thus addressed, spoke to his mother: "I am distressed by the loss of my brothers." 004. The mother replied: "What is the reason for your distress? Who has caused you pain?" 005. He then spoke to her: "I do not know what to do; I am unable to bear it." 006. Thus, the mother, named Śarmā, approached Janamejaya, who was accompanied by his brothers. 007. She, with great affection, said: "This is my son; he has not done anything wrong. What has caused you pain?" 008. Janamejaya, thus addressed, became calm and said: "I have been afflicted by the curse of the sage." 009. He, in that state, was not able to speak; he was overwhelmed by grief. 010. Once, while hunting, Janamejaya, surrounded by his brothers, became lost in the forest. 011. There, he encountered a sage named Ṛṣabha. 012. He then, with great respect, approached the sage. 013. He bowed down and spoke: "O revered one, your son is my brother." 014. Thus addressed, the sage replied: "O Janamejaya, this son of mine has been born from great penance, and he is endowed with divine qualities. May he relieve you of your afflictions." 015. Thus, Janamejaya spoke to the sage: "O revered one, I am afraid." 016. He then took the sage's hand and spoke to his brothers: "Let us proceed."

"Thus, by that which is spoken, the sound of the mother is struck. In the cold, there is a sound. This is your offering. It is the cause of hunger. Thus, he spoke again: He did not speak falsely. Then, the other is the Veda named 'the one who is heavy.' This offering is a command."

The one who is engaged in the right approach is indeed wise. As the seasons change, so let it be. 089 Thus, the sage spoke: "Not by my words is the body made. I am not engaged by the right approach. The one who is endowed with qualities resides in the house. 088 Not to be neglected. 084

56 Pauṇḍav The king said: "Bring the Pauṇḍava! Let him be brought with great honor! He is worthy of respect. Let him be brought! Thus, let the Pauṇḍava be honored. As he is honored, let him be adorned with garlands. Let him be made to shine like a garland of flowers. Indeed, he is the one who is worthy of respect." 100 Thus, he is honored with great respect. He is indeed the one who is worthy of respect. 101 He speaks with great eloquence. He speaks with the eloquence of a sage. 102 Thus, he is indeed worthy of respect. 103 The king said: "Let the worthy one speak." "Do not think ill of him. By the means of respect, he is indeed worthy of honor." 104 Thus, he is indeed the one who is worthy of respect, and he is the one who speaks with great eloquence. 105 He was seated in a respectful manner. He spoke to him with great respect. Then he said: "O revered one, I have come to you." 106 He spoke to him with great respect: "O revered one, what should I do?" 107 He replied: "Let the worthy one be honored with great respect, and let him be adorned with garlands of flowers." 108 The Pauṇḍava said: "With great respect, let the worthy one be honored." 109 Thus, he did not speak with disrespect towards the worthy one. 110

Page 69

003 57 He spoke thus: "Do not be entangled in falsehood. Indeed, you are not bound by the truth. I do not perceive you as being bound." 111 Thus he spoke again: "Your nature is indeed pure. As long as it is so, the truth does not become impure by any means." The father does not become impure by the son. 112 Then, indeed, he spoke: "Even if I am bound by the truth, I will not be affected." 113 The sage spoke: "Thus it is indeed. You will not be affected by the truth." 114 Then, the sage, with a serene countenance, spoke with a gentle voice: "With a pure heart, the truth is indeed pure. The one who is pure will not be affected by the truth." 115 And she, indeed, spoke: "Welcome, O Lord! What should I do?" 116 He replied: "These jewels are to be given to me." 117 She, pleased by this, with a joyful heart, said: "These jewels are indeed yours." 118 And she said: "These jewels belong to the king of serpents. I will not give them." 119 Thus he spoke: "May you be free from all entanglements. Let not the king of serpents harm me." 120

62 Pauṇḍav In that country, at that time, he arrived. Your arrival is known. I am not at fault. Even the best of the best. The great sage is here. 164 Then he began to speak about the means. 003 63 Those who are righteous, they are the rulers of the earth. 172 If the twelve months are completed, then the seasons will be established. That man is a true sage. 64 The guardian of the plants; by their care, the lowly are nurtured. What is the use of a king who does not protect the weak? The wise speak of the king's duty; the virtuous are honored. Thus, the king, with a mind of wisdom; the wise one, having performed his duty, spoke to the king, who was distressed; "Only this body is yours, O king." Those who are not kings, by whom the father is indeed protected; the wise are like the sun, illuminating the world. I am the one who knows the time of death; the great one, having taken the path of righteousness. Thus, the one who is guilty is like a snake; the one who has committed a sin is like a heavy burden. The strong, having taken the path of righteousness, is not without a body; he who is more than ten times your worth. The king, of the lineage of kings, is indeed a protector; he who has killed the wicked is a destroyer of sin. The great sin is indeed destroyed by the one who is righteous; the great king is indeed honored in the world. Thus, having gone to the king, I have been honored; my wish is that the great king is indeed honored. O protector of the earth, by whom I am honored; the great king, having performed his duty, walks without sin. Thus, the king is indeed angry.

**Pauḷomapaṇa** 68 Soot said: By the grace of Bhṛgu's mother, who is endowed with the qualities of Pauḷoma, Bhṛgu was born as a great sage. 011 When the time was right, Bhṛgu, who is of good character, was born in Pauḷoma. 012 For the purpose of anointing, Bhṛgu, the best among those who bear the dharma, then went to Pauḷoma. 013 He, endowed with the qualities of wisdom, was born as Bhṛgu, and with great effort, he was made to shine. 014 However, when Pauḷoma was born, the great sage was also born. At that time, he was nourished by fruits and roots. 015 Then, with great effort, he was made to shine, and he was born as Bhṛgu, who was endowed with wisdom. 016 Then, in the refuge of the sacred fire, the one who is the knower of all was born. 017 Tell me, O sage, what is the truth? 72 Pauḷomapaṁ Even he, by that sin, is not free from doubt. 004 I am indeed the one who knows, Even knowing, you should understand this. 005 Do not be afraid of me. "Thus, the gods, having gathered together, are the protectors of the worlds. The unblemished ones are the inhabitants of the worlds. As the various paths are not obstructed, Thus, the gods, having approached the divine knowledge, The great sage Bhrigu, indeed, is the cause of this. How can the divine face be without a portion? In the world of the great, all beings are connected. But, having spoken the words of the creator of beings, Among all beings, who is the one that sustains the worlds? Thus, in the world, as the sun does not set, When the deluded one, having consumed the essence, Does not sustain the world with all beings, The material cause is the one that is the source of all beings. As the sun, shining brightly, illuminates all, Thus, the essence of all beings is also pure. Therefore, the great light is the source of all beings."

76 Pauḷomapaṇa The marriage is not to be performed for the divine being. | 013 | Then, when the marriage is about to take place, the friends are eagerly waiting for the bride. | 014 | The serpent does not approach the bride, as she is adorned with a beautiful form. | 015 | She, having suddenly fallen to the ground, lost her consciousness, resembling a lifeless body. | 016 | She, being overwhelmed by the time of death, was struck by the poison of the serpent. | 017 | Even though she was in a state of unconsciousness, her beauty became even more enchanting. | 018 | Her father saw her, and so did the others, as they were lamenting on the ground. | 019 | Then, all the assembled people, being filled with compassion, were distressed. | 020 | Bharata, Kaṇṭaka, and Gautama, along with the others, were in the forest. | 021 | O you, who are beautiful, please show mercy and revive her! | 022 | The serpent, having been struck by poison.

Page 89

1. The sage said: Among them, the trees are abundant, the forest is dense, and the river flows. 2. In grief, he did not speak; then he lamented, the lamentation is a source of sorrow. 3. She lies there, my beloved, afflicted by grief; what is not unbearable for all beings? 4. If I have committed a sin, may my suffering be alleviated by the strength of my penance. 5. As the earth bears the burden, so may my grief be borne by the earth. 6. The deity said: By the power of the vow, one should not be afflicted by grief; for the one who has departed, life is not to be lamented. 7. This life is indeed miserable, do not grieve for the departed, do not be troubled. 8. The means of overcoming grief are known; if one is wise, let them be free from sorrow.

1. The sage spoke: My friend was named Khagam, a bird of great wisdom, endowed with the power of austerity. 2. The celestial beings, having gathered there for the sake of immortality, were engaged in austerities. There, the deity Narayana spoke to Brahmā, contemplating thus among the gods. The assembly of gods and demons should churn the ocean, for the future nectar will arise from the great ocean being churned. Having gathered the herbs and all the ingredients, the gods will know the nectar from the churning of the ocean. Sūta said: Then, the mountain Mandara, resembling a peak of clouds, was covered by the wind and surrounded by a net. It was filled with various flocks of birds and crowded with diverse creatures, served by the celestial beings and the nymphs. Rising to an elevation of eleven thousand yojanas, it was established above the earth among thousands of mountains. The gods were unable to approach Vishnu, who was seated there, along with Brahmā.

Page 107

The infinite Lord, from whom Narayana arises, raised the head of the serpent and repeatedly cast it down. From the serpent Vāsuki, the gods, with their smoke and winds, were driven away from the face of the serpent. Those clouds, born from the gathering of smoke, with lightning, overwhelmed the host of gods, causing them distress. From the peaks of the mountain, the flowers rained down, showering the hosts of gods and demons with garlands. A great roar arose here, comparable to the sound of a great storm, as the ocean churned with the gods and demons. There, various aquatic creatures, driven by the great ocean, came to their end in the waters by the hundreds. The beings of Varuna, diverse and dwelling in the depths, were brought to their end. As the mountain was being churned, the beings, filled with joy, fell like birds from the heights of the great trees. From their clash, a fire blazed forth, enveloping the mountain like a blue cloud. The elephants and lions that had emerged were burned, and all beings of various kinds lost their lives.

Page 109

Then the sun, like the ocean, arose with a thousand rays, bright and shining, the moon, the cool and radiant one. After that, the goddess Surā, born from ghee, appeared, and the horse, too, was born, pale in color. The jewel Koṣṭubha, born from the nectar, and the illustrious Nārāyaṇa, emerged from the ocean. From that place, the goddess Śrī, Surā, and the moon, as well as the swift horse, went to the path of the sun. Then the deity Dhanvantari, endowed with a divine form, appeared, holding a white pot where the nectar resides. This is indeed wonderful, as the great roar arose from the demons, speaking of the nectar. Then Nārāyaṇa, assuming a form of illusion, created a marvelous female form, taking refuge among the demons. Then they, with foolish minds, gave him the nectar, all the demons who were intent on that.

The sage said: Thus, I have narrated everything to you, the nectar churned as it is, where the illustrious horse, the mighty Tukhavikrama, was born. Hearing this, Kadru then spoke: O Bhadrā, what color is the horse, Uccaiḥśravā? Please tell me quickly. The one who is bowed down said: This horse is indeed white; do you think otherwise, auspicious one? Please tell me its color, so that it may be brought to the market. Kadru replied: I think this horse is black as a dark cloud; come, let us gamble, O beautiful one, for the sake of the maidservant. The sage said: Thus, having made an arrangement for the maidservant, they went to their own homes, thinking they would see the corpse. That statement was not heeded by the indifferent serpents.

The fearsome one, like a great bird, suddenly arose from the sky. Seeing him, the people took refuge, bowing to the universal form seated there. "O Agni, may you not grow fierce; indeed, this great mass is your lord." Agni spoke: "Do not think that you are like the Asuras; Garuḍa is powerful, equal to my own radiance." Thus addressed, they went to Garuḍa, praising him with words. "You are a sage, a great being, a god, the lord of birds; you are the sun, renowned for your brilliance." "Just as the sun, angry, burns the offspring, so do you burn with the power of the fire." "Fearsome, like Agni, you rise, destroying the cycles of time."

The Sūta said: The birds, urged by the Suparṇa, quickly went to that land, surrounded by the ocean's waters, resounding with the cries of flocks. It was adorned with the colorful flowers of various trees, and also with beautiful lotuses, enveloping the abode. It was embellished with clear waters, like a heart adorned with white jewels, and fragrant with divine scents, blessed by the breeze. The sky was fragrant, even with the scent of the Malaya mountain, and it was beautified by the flowers and the winds. There, it seemed as if the Nagas were showering flowers and water, a delightful place for the minds of the celestial beings and Apsaras. Various birds were singing beautifully, delighting in the playful sounds of the water lilies. Having reached that forest, the serpents joyfully departed, and spoke to the great hero, the excellent bird Suparṇa. "Indeed, you carry us to a beautiful land, where you fly in the sky." Thinking thus, the bird then spoke to his mother Vinatā, "What should I do, mother, that is worthy of discussion?" Vinata spoke.

Page 123

The invincible one among all beings, indeed, is like fire to the Brahman. The angry Brahman becomes like poison to the cloud, the foremost among beings, the father and teacher. Garuda said: As I know the external one, O Mother, you should ask the reason for this. Vinatā said: He who has reached your throat, like a hidden stick, burns like a blazing fire; he should be known as the external one. Sūta said: Vinatā spoke this word to her son, knowing that it is unparalleled and accompanied by great strength. May the wind protect you, and the moon from behind, and may the sun, the great illuminator, protect you. I am always for you, O son, for peace and well-being. May the path be free from obstacles for the sake of achieving your purpose. Then, upon hearing the words of his mother, he spread his wings and soared into the sky. Then, the great time, like the mighty Yama, approached the hungry Nishadas. He then gathered the Nishadas; raising the dust, he greatly stirred the sky, causing the waters of the ocean to surge, contemplating the earth-dwellers nearby.

Page 125

The sage said: Then that Brahmin, having departed with the Nishāda, went to the land after killing the desired Garuḍa. With his wife having departed, that Brahmin, the king of birds, soared into the sky. Then he saw his father and, having inquired, spoke of his father. "I have indeed been sent by my father to bring the moon from the rising." "I will bring him today to free my mother from servitude." "I have been instructed by my mother to eat the Nishādas, but I have not been satisfied after killing thousands." "Therefore, O Lord, it should be declared that I may become capable of consuming the nectar of sacrifice." HATZ said: There was a great sage named Vibhāvasu, who was angry. He had a brother and a younger brother named Supratīka, who was a great ascetic. The great sage did not desire wealth from his brother, who was residing with him. The brother Supratīka, being Vibhāvasu, was indeed very much attached to the division of wealth. Then the brothers, being deluded, did not divide among themselves.

Page 127

He, agitated, caused the lake to be filled with many trees. The tortoise, too, with great vigor, approached for battle. The elephant, cut into six parts, is thus measured from the tip of the trunk. The tortoise's measure is a circle of ten yojanas. These two are prepared for battle, each seeking victory over the other. Using this quickly, let one accomplish the desired task for oneself. Sūta said: Hearing this, Bhīma, swift as the wind, with a single claw, struck the elephant and the tortoise. Then, the bird, having taken flight, approached the divine tree. They, frightened, trembled, struck by the wind from his wings, thinking, "Let us not be harmed," those divine golden branches. The sky-dweller, having moved the other beings, brought forth the desires of the mind. In the golden and silver branches, the ocean's water was reflected, shining great trees. The best of birds spoke to the great Rohiṇī tree, which was very large and descending swiftly. "Here is my great branch, extending a hundred yojanas. Taking this, you should eat, O tortoise."

Page 131

The fire of Indra, accompanied by suffering, blazed forth. The clouds, like the flames of a fire, fell from the sky. Thus, among the Vasus, Rudras, and Ādityas, and the Maruts, as well as other groups of deities, each wielded their respective weapons, clashing with one another. In that unprecedented assembly, even among the gods and demons, the winds gathered, and the meteors fell all around. Even without disturbance, the sky resounded with a great sound. The deity among the gods also became manifest. The divine beings' offerings were like the rays of light, and the clouds of calamity poured forth much blood. The crowns of these beings were raised. Along with the gods, Śatakratu, observing the terrible calamities, spoke to Bṛhaspati. "Why, O Lord, have such great calamities arisen? I do not see any enemy who could defeat us." Bṛhaspati replied, "Due to your offense, O Indra, and your negligence, O Śatakratu, the beings of the Valakhilyas have become agitated by austerity."

Page 135

Upon hearing this, Prajāpati approached the Devarāja and asked about the accomplishment of the ritual. Thus, they replied to him, the truthful ones, saying, "Let it be so." This is Indra's command in the world, created by the power of the gods. The words of the Brahman are not false; the resolve of you all and my own is not false. May Indra, the most powerful among the birds, grant favor as requested by the Devarāja. Thus addressed by Kaśyapa, the ascetics of the forest replied, worshipping the foremost sage, Prajāpati. This endeavor is for Indra; may it be fulfilled for all of us, O Prajāpati. Therefore, this fruitful offering should be accepted by you, and may you arrange it in a way that you see the best outcome. At that very moment, the auspicious goddess Dākṣāyaṇī, known as Vinatā, who desires a son, appeared. Having performed austerities, she approached her husband and spoke to him. This endeavor is indeed meant for you, O goddess, as you desire.

Page 137

The sage said: Then, in that great assembly, the mighty Garuḍa arrived before the gods. He, being exceedingly powerful, caused the surroundings to tremble. There was an immeasurable being, shining like lightning, a great protector of the moon. He, having engaged in battle with the king of birds, was defeated in combat. Shaking off the dust with a great wind, the bird caused the gods to scatter. The gods, covered by the dust, fell into confusion, and they could not see the one who was protecting them. Thus, Garuḍa began to agitate the gods with his beak and wings. Then the god commanded the wind: "O Marut, let this dust storm be dispersed." Then the wind blew away that dust with great force, and the gods regained their clarity.

Page 141

The god spoke to him, "I am the boon-giver, O Khecara." He desired that you may remain above, O one who moves in the sky. He then spoke again to Narayana, "I am imperishable and I shall be like the amṛta even without you." Receiving the boon, Garuḍa spoke to Viṣṇu, "May you also grant a boon; let it be chosen, O Lord." Kṛṣṇa desired that Garuḍa, the mighty one, be his vehicle, and the Lord said, "You will remain above the flag and the discus." Indra struck the bird with a boon, the one who takes away the nectar by force, the friend of the gods. Garuḍa spoke to Indra, "O lord of the falling ones," with a voice that laughed like thunder. "I will perform the vow of the sage, from whom the bones will arise, and I will also perform the vow of the thunderbolt for you, O Rātkṛta." "I abandon this one leaf, for you will not be disturbed by it; for I have no pain at any time from the fall of the thunderbolt." Then all the beings were astonished, seeing the beautiful leaf, "May this one, O Suparṇa, be." "O wondrous one, I wish to know the strength that is your supreme and unparalleled."

Page 143

Garuda said: "Indeed, this moon is being taken by me for some reason. I will not give the moon to anyone." "Wherever I place this thousand-eyed one, you should quickly take it, O Hari." Vākya said: "By this statement, I am pleased, as you have spoken here, O best of birds. Whatever boon you desire from me, I shall grant." Sūta said: "Thus addressed, he replied, remembering the cause of the mother's servitude." "I am indeed the lord of all; I will fulfill your request. The serpents will be my great food." Thus spoken, the slayer of the demons went, saying, "I will take the moon that has been placed." Then, the swift Suparṇa came to the mother, and he spoke this to her, like a great sage: "I will place this nectar that has been brought here among you."

The cause has been stated by you, O Vinatā's son. The boon-giving AA and Kadru and Vinatā as well. The names have been stated for the birds of Vinatā. We wish to hear the names of the serpents, which are not known to you, O Sūta, primarily. Sūta said: Due to their multitude, the names of the serpents are many, O sage. Page 147

Shonaka said: "Are those serpents born, O father, who are difficult to conquer? Knowing that curse, what else did they do?" Soot said: "Having abandoned the fault of those, the great renowned Kadru performed penance, a vow of eating air." "Having reached Gandhamādana and engaged in penance at Vadaya, at Gokarna, at Pushkarāranya, and at the banks of the river." "In those sacred places, in the holy places, being solitary, self-controlled, and constantly conquering the senses." "Seeing the severe penance being performed, the grandfather, the lord with matted hair, addressed him." "The grandfather said to the one performing penance, 'What is this you are doing, O Shesha? Ensure the welfare of the beings.'" "For indeed, by your intense penance, you are tormenting the beings, O sinless one. Speak to me what is in your heart that has long been there." Rage said: "All my brothers are indeed of dull intellect. I do not have the strength to bear this; let it be permitted."

Page 149

Brahmā said: "I am pleased with you; control your anger and act with restraint. This speech of yours is for the welfare of all, as per my instruction." Rage said: "As the god Varada, the lord of creatures, the king of the earth, I shall uphold this steady earth; grant it to me, O Prajāpati, as you desire." Brahmā said: "Descend to the earth, O best of serpents; it will grant you a passage. By holding this earth, you will indeed fulfill my great purpose." Sūta said: "Thus agreeing, the lord, the elder brother of the serpent, stood firm, holding the goddess and this earth, embracing the ocean's edge." Brahmā said: "You are the best of serpents, as you uphold this earth alone; holding the infinite enjoyment, as I have said, you will be powerful." Sūta said: "Thus, the serpent, the infinite one, powerful, descended to the earth."

Page 151

Thus, let us be united here in the name of the serpents. As their honor may be, time has passed. Even if united, the cause of liberation is RATA. As the gods of old have perished, they have entered the cave. As it is, so it may be, or it may also be defeated. For the sake of Janamejaya's sacrifice, indeed for the purpose of destruction. Sūta said: Thus addressed, all the Kadraveyas gathered there. They deliberated there, the wise in the mind. Some there said, "We, having become the serpents, Will not allow Janamejaya's sacrifice to happen." Others, the learned and wise, said, "We will all be in agreement for this matter." He will guide us in all actions or decisions. There we will speak of how the sacrifice should be. He, the king, will consider us all wise. For the sake of the sacrifice, we will say it is not so. Seeing many faults, both here and beyond, By causes and reasons, let the sacrifice not be. Or, the teacher will be the one who will determine what will happen.

Page 155

The serpents, listen! In that place, thus spoken. The wise traveler will become a great sage. Known as Jaratkāra, he is radiant and self-controlled. His son, Jaratkāra, will be born, a great ascetic. A sacrifice named Āstika will then be performed. There, the serpents who will be righteous will be liberated. The gods said: He, the foremost sage, Jaratkāra, the great ascetic, Who will give birth to a great soul? Brahmā said: In the name of Sanāmā, in the name of a maiden, the best of the twice-born, The gods will give birth to a powerful offspring. Thus, the gods spoke to him, the grandfather, saying: Having spoken thus, the gods departed, and he, the god, the grandfather. I see thus, O Vāsuki, your sister, Known as Jaratkāra, I will establish her. For the sake of the fearful, to calm the serpents, O sage of good vows, this liberation has been heard by me.

Page 159

As the Lord Udra pierced the sacrificial deer in the sky, the one with the bow in hand followed, searching here and there. The deer pierced by him does not go to the forest while alive. Indeed, the deer that was pierced by the king was lost. The king, having been far away, was exhausted and thirsty, approached the sage in the forest. In the midst of the cows, having emerged from the mouths of calves, may the best of the milk be drunk by us. The king, with a firm vow, asked the sage, who was afflicted by hunger. O Brahman, I am King Parīkṣit, son of Abhimanyu. I have indeed lost the deer; have you seen it? The sage did not speak anything, remaining silent. The king, having placed a dead serpent on his shoulder, after throwing it aside, did not say anything to him, whether good or bad. The king, abandoning his anger, distressed, went to the city, and the sage remained there. The young son of that king, Bhūttigmateja, was a great ascetic.

Page 161

He, having observed him to be emaciated and having spoken sweet words, asked, "How is it, father? Today, he who bears the burden of death is with me." The player said: "O father, the king, while engaged in the hunt, has been struck down by a serpent on his shoulder." "What wrong has my father done to that king, the wicked one?" "Tell me, you who see the truth, what is the strength of my penance?" The player said: "The king, while hunting, was struck by a single arrow from Parikṣit, and he fell, having been pierced by the bird." "And the king did not pursue another deer in that great forest. He asked his father, who was speaking with him." "That one, who is steadfast and remains, afflicted by hunger and thirst, repeatedly asked your father about the lost deer." The player said: "Then the king, having taken up his bow, shot an arrow at him." "Your father, who is devoted to the vow, is indeed like that. He too, the king, returned to his city, called Gajāhvaya."

Page 163

In the seventh day, that sinful Tākṣaka, the best of serpents, is the leader of the house of Yama, which is extremely dreadful. The father was frightened by him, filled with anger. I have not done anything dear to you, O father; do not punish the ascetics. We reside in the realm of that king. We are protected by him according to justice; I do not like his sin. In every way, the king who is present must always be forgiven by us; for the son of Dharma, when killed, kills. If the king does not protect, then great suffering will come upon us. The son should not act against Dharma, as he wishes. We are protected, O father, by the kings with their branches. We wander in great Dharma; they have a share in it. By the poison of the surrounding, as his great-grandfather protects us, so the subjects must be protected by the king. Today, by that hungry and weary ascetic, this vow has been made without doubt, not knowing. Therefore, this was done by you suddenly in childhood; the king will not curse us, O son, in any way.

Page 165

The world is indeed forgiving; therefore, practice forgiveness constantly. By being forgiving, you will attain the worlds that are immediately after Brahman. But I, having taken to calmness, will do what is possible today; I will send it to the king. You have been cursed by my son with a foolish mind; seeing this destruction of mine, O king, do not be angry. The charioteer said: Thus instructing the disciple, he sent him forth, being virtuous and a great ascetic, compassionate towards the king Parīkṣit. Instructing Kurāṭa about the work and the situation, he sent the one named Gauramukh, who was composed and virtuous. Approaching him quickly, the king entered the palace, as previously informed by the doorkeepers. Having been honored by the king, the brāhmaṇa Gauramukh then told everything to the king, being completely exhausted. The terrible words of the demon, as stated, were indeed spoken. O king, in the region of Shamik, there is a sage of supreme self-control, calm and peaceful, a great ascetic. You, O tiger among men, have been separated from life.

Page 167

Determining the essence of the ministers, he constructed a secure pillar in the palace. He also provided protection there, along with the physicians and herbs, and the Brahmins invoked the perfected mantra from all sides. The one residing there, being a king, shone like his own jewels, surrounded by knowledgeable ones and well-protected from all sides. On the seventh day, the best of the twice-born approached the king to treat him. For he had heard that the best of kings, Tākṣaka, would not be taken to Yama's abode. "I will deal with the king of serpents, Tākṣaka, regarding the fever," he thought. That Tākṣaka, the king of serpents, was going along the path, becoming a twice-born, with a single mind. The king of serpents, Tākṣaka, approached the great sage, saying, "What work do you wish to accomplish quickly?" The speaker said: "The king born of the Kuru lineage, Parīkṣit, will be consumed by the great Tākṣaka, the best of serpents, with his fiery energy." The one bitten by the king of serpents, with that same fiery energy, was the king of the Pāṇḍavas, of immeasurable glory.

Page 169

The sage said: Thus addressed, the great serpent approached the fig tree, having been seen by the great sage Kaśyapa. That tree, having been bitten by him, immediately blazed with great splendor. Having burned it, the serpent spoke again: "O best of the birds, revive this tree." Having turned the tree to ashes with the brilliance of the serpent king, he spoke the following words. "O serpent king, I will revive this tree, which is in this state of ashes; look at it, O serpent." Then the Lord, the best of the twice-born, revived the tree that had been turned to ashes with his knowledge. Thus, he, accompanied by the serpent, revived the tree with branches and leaves. The great sage Kaśyapa, seeing the tree revived, spoke to Takṣaka: "This is indeed wonderful for you." "O best of the brāhmaṇas, whatever you desire to be destroyed, whether mine or that of my kind, who do you wish to harm there, O ascetic?" "Whatever fruit you desire, I will give it to you, even if it is from the best of kings."

Page 171

The king, protected by the mantra, contemplated then, "How can I deceive this prince through my illusion?" Then, in the form of a sage, he sent the serpents, taking the water from the leaf to the king, and thus Tākṣaka spoke. "Go, you who are untroubled, to the king to receive the water from the leaf." The charioteer said: They, instructed by Tākṣaka, did as the serpents did, and they brought fruits to the king. Thus, the king, the mighty one, received everything, and told them to go. When those serpents, disguised as sages, had gone, the king spoke to his ministers and friends: "Let you all eat these sweet fruits, which I have brought from the sage." Then the king, along with his minister, wished to take the fruits, and there a worm was found in the fruit taken by the king. A dark-eyed man of reddish hue, of the color of a red gem.

Page 175

Thus, let them attain everything through my austerity. Let them be fulfilled in their desires. The fathers said: O blessed one, the one who desires to eat the flesh of others, cannot be freed from the consequences of austerity. Let us know the fruit of austerity, O best one, from the destruction of progeny, we fall into hell, impure. Here, let us not be ignorant, by which we do not recognize you, the renowned man in the world. O blessed one, the great fortune, who should be mourned by us, the very miserable. We, the sages named Yāyāvara, have fallen from the world due to our pious deeds, from the destruction of progeny. Our lost austerity is not present, there is indeed no progeny for us. There is one who is our progeny, but he is also not there. The unfortunate one, of little fortune, is indeed our relative. He is known as Jaradkaru, well-versed in the Vedas and their branches. The self-controlled great soul, with excellent vows and great austerity. Thus, we are indeed afflicted by the austerity. There is no son or relative of his.

Page 181

As the moon grows in the Shungapakshe, so does he. Then, after a few hours, Jarātkaru, the great ascetic, having made the head of this one his resting place, slept, being exhausted. While he was asleep, the best of the brāhmaṇas, Savitā, approached him. At dawn, Brahman thought deeply. Vāsuki's sister, frightened by the loss of her strength, was distressed. What good deed may I have done that I may not be afflicted by this sorrow? How can I not be troubled by this offense? Is anger or the loss of virtue greater? Thus, the mind became agitated. I will raise him; who will create this unwavering anger? This will surely happen at the time of twilight. Thus, having resolved in his mind, Jarātkaru, the serpent, spoke to the sage, who was radiant and lying without loss. He spoke this sweet word: "Arise, O great one, the sun is setting. Worship at twilight, O Lord, for you are steadfast." Having been disturbed, the one who is the great ascetic, Jarātkaru,

Page 183

The speech is dry from the mouth; with folded hands, the eyes are filled with tears. You do not deserve to abandon me, O knower of dharma. Established in dharma, always engaged in what is dear and beneficial. The reason for the donation, O best of the brāhmaṇas, is that I have obtained a poor one; what will Vāsuki say to me? O best one, afflicted by the curse of my mother, my relatives desire offspring from you. For by obtaining offspring from you, my relatives will have joy; this connection will not be futile for me, O twice-born. Wishing for the welfare of my relatives, I pray to you, O Lord; please accept this unmanifest form of mine. How can the great soul abandon me, desiring to connect with the unworthy? Thus addressed, he spoke to the sage; whatever is appropriate and logical, Jaradkaru, the ascetic, said. There is indeed a great one, O fortunate one, like you, the universal form; the sage is of supreme dharma, well-versed in the Vedas and their branches. Having spoken thus, the dharmic sage Jaradkaru, the great sage, went to perform greater austerities with determination.

Page 185

Jarātkaru then spoke, comforting the tormented Vāsuki, the lord of serpents. Questioned about the reason for my son, that great soul with great austerity, he said, "Indeed, this is my fate." I do not remember anything false about him. O king, where will he speak of the future? There is no distress for you regarding the serpent; your son will surely rise like a blazing fire. Thus speaking, he went to the hermitage of his brother, where this great sorrow resides in your mind. Hearing this, the lord of serpents, Vāsuki, was filled with great joy, and he accepted that statement from his sister. With soothing words, gifts, and appropriate worship, the excellent serpent honored his sister. Then, the embryo of great radiance, like the sun, began to grow. In the same manner, she, the daughter of Brahmā, gave birth to a son, free from the fear of father and mother. He grew there in the abode of the king of serpents, and he mastered the Vedas and the sacred texts. A child of virtuous conduct, endowed with intelligence and qualities.

Page 187

Hearing from you, indeed, in the anger of a father, I shall attain auspiciousness; never shall it be the opposite. The Sūta said: Then the great-souled one spoke this word, being questioned by that great soul, the wise one, knowing his own duty, to King Janamejaya. Thus, the great-souled one, the protector of the people, your father, was indeed as he was, that great-souled one. Having established the four varṇas in their own duties, he protected them; the king, knowing dharma, was indeed a warrior of dharma. He protected the earth, the goddess, the glorious one of unmatched prowess; he does not hate anyone, nor does he desire anything. He was equal towards all beings, like Prajāpati. The brāhmaṇas, kṣatriyas, and vaiśyas, and also the śūdras, were established in their own duties, being well-disposed towards the king, by that king's own conduct. He bore the burden of the helpless, the poor, and the wretched; he was like the moon among all beings. The prosperous one, endowed with truthfulness and great prowess, was a disciple in the science of archery, that king. Govinda was dear to him, your father Janamejaya, and he was dear to all the people, the great one of great fame.

Page 188

176 Āstīkapaṃva In the Kurus, in the northern region, Parīkṣit was born; he was the son of Subhadrā. The king, skilled with the axe, was self-controlled and wise, served by the elderly. The great sage, knowledgeable in ethics and dharma, protected the people for sixty years. Then, having attained a certain fate, he was bitten by a serpent. Therefore, you, the best of men, should uphold dharma; this kingdom has come to the Kurus for a thousand years. You are still a child, a protector of your own kind. Janamejaya said: In this lineage, there has never been a king who did not act for the welfare of the people, especially considering the deeds of the great-grandfathers, who were devoted to the highest good. How did my father meet his end? Please tell me as it is; I wish to hear the truth. Sūta said: Thus addressed by the king, the ministers spoke to the king, devoted to the welfare of all. Your father became a hunter, O king, just like the great Bhīma, the best archer in battle.

Page 189

As we are all angry with the king. Once, a hunter shot a deer with an arrow. The deer, struck, fled into the dense forest. The deer, bound by the hunter's arrow, could not escape; your father did not catch it. Exhausted and aged, he was sixty years old, afflicted by hunger, he saw a sage in the great forest. The king asked the sage, who was observing silence. The sage did not speak even when questioned. Then the king, filled with hunger, approached the sage, who was standing still, observing silence, and calm, immediately overtaken by anger. The king did not recognize the sage, who was observing silence. Overcome by anger, he struck his father. The dead serpent was lifted from the ground by the best of Bharata. The wise one did not speak to him, whether good or bad. He stood there, not angry, holding the serpent with his arm.

Page 191

Father, having made a pact with you, shines like a flame. The one who, in the presence of the guru, releases the dead, that Nāga Taksaka, enraged, will strike with his brilliance. From the seventh night onwards, the sin has been a burden on my penance. Thus speaking, he went there where his father was. The six also informed the father of the curse. And that sage, having sent to your father, said, "You are cursed by my son, O king." Taksaka will strike you down, O great king, with his brilliance. Hearing those terrible words, your father Janamejaya was terrified of Taksaka, the best of serpents. Then, on the seventh day, he wished to go near the king. The king of serpents, having given him, made Taksaka act. The newly arrived king of serpents hurriedly went. "Why do you hurry, what work do you wish to accomplish?" Kāiyapa said, "Where the king, the best of the Kurus, is surrounded, he will surely be burned by Taksaka, the serpent."

Janamejaya said: "I wish to hear this, about the conversation of the king of serpents and Karayapa that took place in the solitary forest." "By whom has this been heard, and has it come to your ears? After hearing, I will establish the understanding of the serpent's end." The ministers spoke: "Listen, O king, how this was narrated before, about the meeting of the best of the twice-born and the king of serpents on the path." "In that tree, a certain man, seeking fuel, was searching for a dry branch of the plant." "While he was searching, the serpent-born twice-born was there in the tree." "Then, he was burned by that tree, and due to the power of the twice-born, the plant was alive, O king." "Thus, having gone, it was reported in this city." "All that happened, as it is, of the serpent and the twice-born." "This has been told to you, O king, as it was heard." "After hearing, O king, act as you desire." The sūta said: "After hearing the words of the ministers, King Janamejaya, suffering from grief, pressed his hand."

Page 195

The sage said: Thus speaking, the illustrious one, having been approved by me, ascended the vow, the king Parthiva. At that time, the king Parīkṣit, who was a Brahmin by birth, was present. The king summoned the priest and the officiating priest of the earth. Whoever has killed my father, that wicked Tākṣaka, let them speak as they wish. If that deed is known to you by which I will obtain Tākṣaka, the serpent, I will do so with my relatives. As my father was previously burned by poison, I too wish to burn that sin, the serpent. Priests: O king, there is a great sacrifice for you, the divine one, which is known as the great sacrifice. There is no other king who can harm that sacrifice; thus, the ancient ones have said, and we also have this capability. The sage said:

Page 197

Then the rituals commenced according to the prescribed method. The priests, having donned the garments of Krishna, with smoke-red eyes, offered the oblations as if they were the fire itself. They agitated the minds of all the serpents there; the priests offered the oblations facing the fire. Then the offerings were completed, and the blazing sacrificial fire, being wrapped up, called out to each other in distress. They were flashing and breathing heavily, and they covered the gas with their heads. The white, black, and blue ones, the aged ones, roared with the sounds of Bhairava, O Lord of the universe. Thus, hundreds of thousands of serpents, the best of the twice-born, were destroyed. Some were like the moon, others like elephants, and some were intoxicated like great elephants, large-bodied and powerful. Many were high and low, of various forms, fierce and resembling the club of a hammer, very powerful. They fell down, the serpents, tormented by the words of their mother.

Page 199

CARA and the great Maudraka, the one with the fierce vow. Many others, including these, were external vow-takers. They were present as members in the sacrifice of Parīkṣit. At that time, the great Kato, while performing the sacrifice, was overwhelmed by terrifying creatures. The waters of the rivers, which are known as the Nāgas, were filled with fragrance and tumult. Let the sound of the falling Nāgas, who are established in the sky, be heard loudly. Tākhaka, the king of snakes, went to the abode of the king after hearing about the consecrated Janamejaya. Then, the great serpent narrated everything as it happened and went to the shelter of the fearful Purandara. Indra spoke to him, pleased: "O Tākhaka, do not fear here. The fear of the serpent sacrifice is not to be taken lightly." Previously, I was pleased for your sake, O grandfather. Therefore, you have no fear; let your mental fever depart. Thus reassured by him, the great serpent resided happily in the abode of Shakra. Vasuki, who was constantly falling into grief in the mountains, was troubled by minor faults.

Page 201

Vasuki, the king of serpents, spoke thus. "I am your father's son, given by my brother for a purpose. This is the situation; do as you see fit." Āstīka said: "For what reason did your father give me to my maternal uncle? Please tell me the truth; having heard it, I will act accordingly." Sūta said: "She spoke thus to him, being a well-wisher of the relatives, the sister of the serpent king Jarātkaru." "The mother of all serpents is known as Kadru, as heard. By her curse, her son was born, so know that." "Uccaiḥśravā, the horse king, was not created by me falsely. For the sake of a humble offering, O sons, you will become servants." "In the sacrifice of Janamejaya, the wind-rider will burn you. There, having attained the fifth state, you will go to the realm of the dead." "Thus, having been seized, the grandfather of the world spoke. 'So be it,' he said, and he was pleased with that statement." "And Vasuki, having heard the words of the grandfather, went to the nectar churned, O father, to the abode of the gods." "All the perfected beings and the gods will attain the supreme nectar."

Page 203

I will liberate you, O best of serpents, Vāsuki. Therefore, I will fulfill this promise to you. May you be free from fear, O noble serpent; indeed, you have nothing to fear. I will strive, O gentle one, for your well-being. My words are not false; how could they be otherwise, even in the morning? Going to that king, Janamejaya, who is initiated, I will please him with words filled with my essence, O mother. As that sacrifice will be fulfilled by the king, so shall it be. Thus, O great-minded serpent king, everything is possible for me. Your mind will never be false towards me. Vāsuki said: O Āstīka, my heart is filled with anguish; I do not know the fate of the people, being tormented by the rod of Brahman. Āstīka replied: You should not be troubled, O best of serpents; I will not let the fear born from the blazing fire harm you. I will destroy the terrible rod of Brahman, which is as fierce as the fire of time; you will not suffer any fear from me. Sūta said:

Page 205

The sacrifice of Nṛga and that of Ajāmīḍha; as the sacrifice of Daśaratha the king. Thus is this sacrifice of yours, O Bhārata; may there be well-being for Parīkṣit and the dear ones. The sacrifice of Kṛṣṇa, the son of Satyavatī; where he himself performed the action. Thus is this sacrifice of yours, O Bhārata; may there be well-being for Parīkṣit and the dear ones. In the fire of your sacrifice, the one who is the destroyer of Vṛtra; how does it unite? Now, knowledge is to be known; to those who have given, let it not be taken away. There is no one like the ṛtviks in the worlds; this is determined by the Dvaipāyana. Indeed, the ṛṣis wander the earth; the priests are skilled in their own duties. The glorious one, the sun with its diverse rays; the one who consumes the offerings, O deity, is blazing. There is no other being in the world; equal to the king, the protector of the people. With determination and a joyful mind, I am always; you are either the king, Dharma, or Yama. You are indeed the one who is the protector of the people; in this world, there is no other householder in the sacrifice. The one who is like the great Yayāti; with the same influence as Māndhātṛ. The brilliance of the sun is like your brilliance; O Bhīṣma, as you shine, you are the one of great vows. Like Vālmīki, hidden in the forest; like Vasiṣṭha, who is ever controlled in anger. My opinion is that your power is equal to Indra's; and your radiance shines like that of Nārāyaṇa. As Yama is the one who determines dharma; Kṛṣṇa is endowed with all virtues.

196 Āstīkapaṃva This one comes, O king, like a thunderbolt; A great roar is heard from him, terrifying like Bhairava. Surely, the serpent, having been freed from the grip of the thunderbolt, Is now in a state of confusion, losing its identity; the great serpent king breathes heavily. O king, it is indeed your duty to give a boon to the foremost of the twice-born. Janamejaya said: I wish to grant you a boon that is immeasurable in beauty; Choose what is in your heart, and I shall give it to you, even if it is difficult to grant. Sūta said: While the serpent king Takṣaka was falling, Āstīka urged him thus. If you grant a boon, I ask for a drink; Let your sacrifice not be disturbed by the serpents here. Thus addressed, King Parīkṣit was not overly joyful, And he spoke these words to Āstīka. Gold, silver, and whatever else you desire, O wise one, I shall give you a boon, let it not be turned back. Āstīka said: I do not ask for gold, silver, or anything else from you, O king.

Then the sound of the plough was heard, and the joyful Astīka was present. The king Janamejaya, son of the Pandava Parīkṣit, was filled with joy. To the priests and the assembled members who were present there, he gave wealth in thousands. To the red-eyed Sūta and the architect, the Lord spoke of the offering of the sacrifice. For the sake of the Brahman, he gave much wealth to him, and then he performed the ritual as prescribed. Astīka was sent to the well-treated home, and the king, with a joyful heart, honored the wise one. He spoke to him, saying, "You must return; you will be a member of my great sacrifice." Thus instructed, Astīka joyfully fled, having accomplished his task and pleased the king. Having gone, he was extremely pleased and approached his mother, Matṛ, and reported everything as it happened. Hearing this, those who were present, free from delusion, became joyful; they said to Astīka, "Choose a boon that you desire."

He who gave birth to Pāṇḍu, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and Vidura, the great sage of the Kuru lineage. At that time, Janamejaya, the royal sage, entered the sacrificial hall accompanied by those well-versed in the Vedas and their branches. There, he saw the king seated, surrounded by many members, like the lord of the gods. Thus, with the sages and the rulers of various regions, the priests skilled in the divine rites performed the great sacrifice. Janamejaya, the best of the Bharatas, joyfully rose with his retinue upon the arrival of the sage. The lord, with the consent of the assembly, arranged a golden seat for him, like that of Brihaspati. There, the king worshipped the sage, who was seated and purified by the assembly of divine sages, with a focused mind. He offered water for washing the feet, the ceremonial water, and the cow according to the prescribed rites to his grandfather Krishna, as was fitting. After fully worshipping him, he sat down and, with a joyful heart, asked him about the name of the river. The Lord also, having seen him, skillfully responded.

206 Worshipped by the members, he did not worship the members. Then, all the members honored Janamejaya. After this, with folded hands, he asked about the great deeds of the Kurus and the Pandavas. Among all the grandfathers, whose minds are filled with divine presence, O Bhagavan, are you well? Hearing his words, then Krishna Dvaipayana was seated near the sage. The sage said: Having worshipped the guru with mind, intellect, and meditation, and having honored all the twice-born, as well as others who are knowledgeable. I shall speak the opinion of Vyāsa, the one of immense splendor, for the benefit of all in the world. Listen, O King, to this story of Bhāratī, which you have attained; the words of the guru resonate with joy and inspire me. Hear, O King, how it was between the Kuru and Pāṇḍava for the sake of the kingdom; thus the forest exile came to be. And how the war became a cause for the destruction of the earth, I shall explain to you, O best of the Bharatas, as you inquire. When your father died, the heroes came from the forest to their own abode, and soon the learned ones became skilled in the Vedas and archery. Thus, those endowed with beauty and valor, who were well-versed and approved by the people, did not envy the Pāṇḍavas, who were renowned and glorious. Then Duryodhana, the wicked, along with Karṇa and others, engaged in various actions to subdue them. The sinful one gave poison to Bhīma, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and that hero, with the help of food, overcame it.

208 Adi Vamsha Avatarana In that place, Bhīma, having been bound by Gad, was thrown into the waters of the Ganges and went to the city. When the son of Kunti became aware, he cut the bonds and rose up, the great king Bhīma, free from pain. If he was asleep, struck by the venom of the serpent Krishna, he did not die, even in all the commands of the enemies. Among those wise ones, in those matters, Vidura became alert regarding their liberation and opposition. Just as the sweetener of sugar is beneficial for the living world, so too is Vidura always beneficial for the Pandavas. When various means were employed, even with the revealed ones, the Narakas, who were protected by divine beings, were killed. Then, with ten ministers like Duhshasana, having obtained permission from Dhṛtarāṣṭra, he ordered the house of the Pandavas. There, he made the Pandavas dwell, and did not burn them with fire again. By the words of Vidura, the miner was then instructed, and they were liberated through yoga, fleeing in fear. Then, in the great forest, there was a demon named Hidiṃba, whom Bhīma, enraged, killed with his great prowess. Then, the heroes, having made a pact, began to prepare the chariot.

Thus, they were devoted to dharma, committed to truth, and arose without negligence, shining brightly. Ajaya, the son of Dhīmasena, was indeed mighty in the east; the brave Sāuno was in the north, and Nakula was in the west. In the south, Sahadeva conquered the enemy warriors. Thus, they all made this earth flourish.

This indeed is the pure and supreme assembly of the Vedas, the best among the audible, praised by the sages in the Purāṇa. In this great history of merit, wisdom is also the ultimate goal. Those who are devoid of faith, who are deceitful, and who are untruthful, should listen to this Veda. The sin of killing a fetus is indeed a great sin; without doubt, upon hearing this history, even a person of great misdeeds should listen to it, for it is called victory. One who conquers the earth conquers all enemies as well. This is the best Pumsavana, this is the great Swastyayana. It should be heard from the great kings. This supreme Dharma, the means of liberation, is proclaimed by Vyāsa, the one of great wisdom. Currently, they will narrate, and others will also tell. There are sons who are devoted to service and messengers who are dear doers. The sin committed by the body, by speech, and by the mind, all of it is quickly abandoned by a man who always listens. For the people of Bhārata, there is no fear of disease, nor any fear of the afterlife. Blessed are those with fame, longevity, and good fortune.

And whoever recites this from the beginning, his ancestors are exalted and he attains immortality. Whatever a man does in ignorance, upon hearing this great Mahābhārata, he is liberated. The great birth of the Bharatas is called the Mahābhārata; whoever knows its meaning is freed from all sins. Vērāmpāyana said: There was a king named Dhamanitya, the ruler of the earth, who once went hunting. He, the son of Vasu, the delight of Paurava, was to be captured by Indra, the king of the gods. The god himself, having taken the form of a sage, approached the king who was residing in the hermitage. Considering that the king was worthy of kingship due to his austerities, he directly inspired the king with his grace. Indra said: This dharma is not to be neglected on earth, O king of the earth. Protect this dharma, for it indeed sustains the world. You, being constantly engaged in righteous actions, will attain the eternal merits. You are my dear friend, residing in the land of the earth. May you, being virtuous and prosperous, enjoy the qualities of the land. This land is indeed filled with wealth and resources.

The king took the beloved daughter of Girikā. The wife of Vasa, Girikā, offered herself due to desire. On that day, the parents said to him, "You are like a deer." Remembering Girikā, the best of kings went hunting, not deviating from the command of his ancestors. She was exceedingly beautiful, like a goddess. His seed fell while wandering in the beautiful forest. The king thought, "Let my seed not fall in vain," and "Let the season not be unfruitful for her." The king, contemplating this, repeatedly thought about the infallibility of his seed. Seeing the wood for the offering of the seed, he approached Abhimanyu, who was standing there. Knowing the subtlety of the matter, he spoke to the falcon. "Take this seed, my gentle one, to my home quickly, and give it to Girikā."

220 Grabbing it, the swift hawk took off, relying on supreme speed, the bird fled. Then, the hawk, having reached the other side, was immediately attacked by the fish-eating creature. In the battle of beaks, both engaged in a fierce struggle, and that also fell into the waters of the Yamunā. There, known as Adrika, the nymph, having received a curse from Brahmā, became a resident of the Yamunā. The fish, having lost its hawk-like feet, then approached Vāsava, saying, "O fish-shaped one, come quickly!" Once, the fish, having bound the fish-eater, when the month of Dāra arrived, then the best of the Bharatas. From Rudra, it was said that she was a human. Realizing the wonder, he reported to the king, "O king, these fish are indeed born as humans." Then, the king captured the male fish, who was named Rājāsīddhāmin, the true conqueror. The nymph, freed from the curse, immediately attained her form, as previously stated by the Lord, having fallen into a fortunate birth. Having given birth to a human, you will attain the release from the curse. The descent of the original lineage.
