There, Mādri ascended the chariot. She spoke to Kuntī: "By the carelessness of the two, it must happen." Then the five Pāṇḍavas, along with Kuntī, were brought to Hastinapura, as informed by the ascetic Bhīṣma and Vidura. Even there, they could not burn the Jatugṛha due to Vidura's warning. Then, having killed Hidiṃba, they went to Ekachakra. In that Ekachakra, having killed a demon named Baka, they reached Pāścālana city. From there, they found Draupadī and returned to their own place. The letters were produced. Yudhiṣṭhira was (the son of) Kunti. Vṛkodara was (the son of) Sutasoma. Ajuna was (the son of) Śrutakīrti. Nakul was (the son of) Śatānīka. Sahadeva was (the son of) Taha. Yudhiṣṭhira, however, in the svayaṃvara of the goddess Devikā, gave birth to a son named Yodheya. Bhīmasena also took a wife named Barudhara, who was of valor. From her, he gave birth to a son named Savega. Ajana, indeed, going to Dvāravatī, married the sister of Vāsudeva, named Subhadrā. From her, he gave birth to a son named Abhimanyu. Nakul, too, married a wife named Vai Kareṇuvatī. From her, he begot a son named Niramitra. Sahadeva also took Mādri in her svayaṃvara, marrying a wife named Vijayā. From her, he gave birth to a son named Suhotra. Bhīmasena, earlier, had a son named Ghaṭotkaca from the demon Hidiṃbā. Thus, these are the eleven sons of the Pāṇḍavas. From the daughter of Virāṭa, he married a wife named Uttarā. From her, a son named Parāśu was born.

Page 383

The abode of her was raised by Maruta. Then the host of gods, with great speed, came together. The great sage, King Śaṅka, saw the river. The great sage, having been addressed by the Lord Brahmā, was born among mortals. Thinking thus, the king, along with the kings and the great sages, pleased his father with abundant words. The great sage, however, was to be taken by the river, which was not to be forsaken. She, being fearful of your form, was struck by the hand of the divine. While going on the path, the gods of the heavens were present. Thus, the river asked the best of the rivers, "What is this, O shapeless ones? Is there any welfare for the heavenly beings?" The gods replied to her, "We are indeed caught in the great river due to a slight offense caused by the great sage Vasiṣṭha." Indeed, we are all bewildered, concealed from the best of sages. In the evening, we saw Vasiṣṭha seated, and we were previously driven away by him. Therefore, out of anger, we are unable to be born. It cannot be otherwise, as stated by the great sage. You, having become human, should give birth to sons, O Vasu. We do not enter the womb of mortals, for we are inauspicious.

Page 384

Thus spoke the Ganges to the sons of Vasu: "O best of men, who among you will be born?" The Vasu said: "The son of Pratīpa, the righteous king of Śantanu, will be born among men. What will happen to us?" Gadā said: "The gods also think like this; I will do what is dear to him, if you agree." The Vasu said: "You should not be born as a son; otherwise, there will be no release from the world." Gadā said: "I will do this; let the son be born according to that. There will be no futile connection with me." The Vasu said: "We will grant the strength of each one of us; through that strength, your son will be born as desired." He will not prosper among men, but your son will be strong. Therefore, a son will be born to you, who will be strong.

Page 386

I do not desire another's wealth, O beautiful one. Know that I do not wish for anything else. Khyuvāca: I am not a serpent, nor should anything be spoken. Worship me, O king, worship the maiden. Pratīpa uvāca: This is what you have asked of me, which is dear to me. Otherwise, the one who is determined would not be destroyed. Having reached my southern thigh, you are the beautiful maiden. Let the seat be arranged for the daughters-in-law of the timid. On the left side, the part of the beloved is rejected by you. Therefore, I shall not act with desire towards you, O beautiful maiden. May you be well, O auspicious one; I choose you for the sake of a son. You are indeed the left thigh, having come and taken refuge. Khyuvāca: Even so, I shall be united with your son, O knower of righteousness. For your sake, I shall worship the renowned abode of Bhārata. Among those who are rulers on earth, you are the ultimate. I cannot express my qualities even in a hundred years. Saṃbhavapavaṁ

Page 387

The one who has departed from the house is indeed the best among the excellent. One should not act in a way that is contrary to one's noble birth. Your son will attain his own through virtuous and beloved letters. Thus, having spoken, she vanished from the king's sight. The king, waiting for the birth of a son, held on to that. In this very moment, the Kṣatriya Pratīpa performed austerities for the sake of his son, O descendant of the Kurus. From them was born a son, the great Bhīṣma, who was born of Śāntanu. Remembering the sorrows conquered by his own actions, the noble one of the Kurus became a divine being. Pratīpa, the son of Śāntanu, then followed the youth. If she were to approach you secretly, she would be a daughter of excellent qualities. She should not be spoken of by you, who is she and whose is she? Whatever should be done, that should not be questioned by you, O sinless one.

Due to my vow, I shall worship that being. | 022 | Thus instructing his son, then Pratīpa entered the forest, having anointed him as king in his own kingdom. | 023 | That king, the wise Śantanu, known on earth as a great archer, became a hunter, constantly roaming the forest. | 024 | He, the best of kings, hunted deer and buffalo, wandering along the Ganges, served by Siddhas and Charanas. | 025 | Once, the great king saw a woman shining like a lotus, with a form radiant and beautiful. | 026 | She was flawless, adorned with divine ornaments, holding a fine garment, and glowing like the essence of a lotus. | 027 | Seeing her, the king was astonished, his body covered with goosebumps, and he was not satisfied merely by gazing at her. | 028 | She, while wandering, did not satisfy the king, who was captivated by her great beauty. | 029 | Then the king spoke to her with a soothing voice: "Are you a goddess, a demoness, a celestial nymph, or a human of exquisite beauty? | 030 | Are you a Yakṣiṇī, a serpent, or a beautiful human? Or are you the one who brings delight to the gods, my beautiful one?" | 031 | Hearing the words of the king, she smiled sweetly and, remembering the time of the Vasus, approached him without blame. | 032 | Saṁbhavapavaṁ

Page 389

The queen spoke to the king with a heart-melting voice: "I will be your devoted wife, O ruler of the earth." As for the king, whether it be the son of the king or the son of the noble, I will not be prevented from speaking what is dear to me. Thus, while speaking in this manner, I will remain with you, O prince, and I will abandon what is unpleasant. Thus, the queen was addressed by the king, and then the best of the Bharatas smiled, having received her. Approaching, Śantanu enjoyed her as he desired, thinking that he should not ask her anything, nor did he say anything to her. By her virtuous character and her beauty, she pleased the lord of the worlds. That divine goddess, Ganges, having taken a human form, became the most beautiful one. For the one desiring good fortune, she became the wife of the lion among kings, equal to the brilliance of the king of gods. He delighted the king as he delighted himself. The king, being attached to pleasure and endowed with excellent qualities, did not notice the passing of many years and seasons.

378 Possible Purification The lord of the people, enjoying with her, gave birth to a son as desired. 043 As soon as she gave birth, she cast the child into the water, O Bhārata. I am pleased with you, she said, and with the strength of the mace, he was victorious. 044 Then, that which was dear to the king became a source of lamentation. The king, frightened by abandonment, did not speak to her at all. 045 Then, when the eighth son was born, as if smiling, the king, in sorrow, spoke to his son, wishing for him. 046 "Do not kill! Who are you? Why do you harm your sons? O son-killer, do not attain such a great sin; do not remain condemned." 047 He spoke: "I do not kill my son, O son of the best of sons. Having given birth to the eight Vasus, you have conquered the worlds, O imperishable one. 052

Page 392

Varuṇa, the son of the ocean, once obtained the best of the Bharata lineage. The sage named Vasiṣṭha, renowned for his prowess, was also known as Āpava. His hermitage was sacred, accompanied by a multitude of deer and birds, adorned with flowers from the side of Mount Meru, the king of mountains. Varuṇa performed austerities there, O best of the Bharatas, in the forest rich with the fruits and waters of virtuous deeds. The daughter of Dakṣa, known as Surabhī, was greatly esteemed, and from her, the goddess Gāṁ was born. To bestow blessings upon the world, Varuṇa, the lord of the waters, brought forth the divine cow, the wish-fulfilling cow, Gāṁ. She resided in that forest of ascetics, served by sages, and roamed in the beautiful and righteous places, free from fear. Then, at that moment, the best of the Bharatas arrived. The Vasus, led by Prithvī, were present there. They, along with their wives, wandered in that forest, enjoying the delightful and pleasant places. In that forest, one of them, while wandering, gave a beautiful cow to the sage Vasiṣṭha, who was the best among those who fulfill desires. She, filled with wonder, possessed the wealth of character and virtue, and during the day, she revealed that cow to the bull-eyed Gāṁ. The pure one appeared.

Page 393

The auspicious one with a beautiful face, endowed with excellent qualities, is connected to the divine Vasudeva. O king, in the past, there was a city known for its inhabitants. Then, the king spoke to that goddess, describing her beauty and qualities. This goddess is the most beautiful, with eyes like the Varuṇa. Whoever drinks her milk will live with youthful stability. Hearing this, the goddess, with a beautiful form, spoke to the excellent king. In the human world, I have a friend, a daughter of the gods, named Jinavatī, who is beautiful and youthful. O king, the daughter of the wise and truthful one is renowned in the world for her beauty. For her sake, bring my desired cow quickly, O best of the auspicious. As long as she drinks this milk, may she be the only one among humans, free from old age and disease.

382 Possible Purity This is my great fortune, for I am not at all insulted by the great one. There is nothing dearer to me than this. Hearing these words from the goddess, desiring to please her, the Earth took away that village from the two. Then, appointed by the lotus-eyed one, the king could not see the sage's intense penance. That one was taken away by him; the waterfall was not to be reasoned about. Then, having reached the hermitage and having taken fruits, the Varuṇa did not go there to the best of the forest. Then he began to hunt in the forest, the one who was a great ascetic did not go to hunt that village. Knowing that it had been taken away, the divine seer, having compassion, cursed the Vasus. From whom the Vasus, having taken the cow, will be born among men. Thus, the Lord cursed the Vasus, the best of the sages, having received the wrath of the great one, O Bharata. And he, the great one, fixed his mind on penance. Thus, he cursed the king, the Vasus were lost, the ascetic. The great power of the Lord was accompanied by the anger of the gods.

Page 395

Having reached the hermitage, the great-souled one was addressed by the sages, who said, "We know that you have cursed us." The Vasus, seeking to please the sage, did not reject the boon from the best of sages. The man-tiger, having torn apart the wind, spoke. He, the righteous one, said, "You seven, O Earth-dwellers, after a year, will attain liberation from the curse." But for the sake of which you have been cursed by me, he will live long in the human realm by his own actions. I do not wish for falsehood; I am angry with you for what you have said. The great-minded one will not be born among humans. The righteous one, skilled in his own weapon, will act for the welfare of his father and will enjoy the pleasures of companionship. Thus speaking, the sage departed. Then all the Vasus approached me and requested a boon, which I granted. Having been born, we are cast into the waters. Thus, I, having properly fulfilled the desires of the righteous, have acted for the liberation of the human beings. This is the only curse of the best of kings.

Page 397

The one who is superior to all due to desire, thus established. Among these, the great one, Śantanu, the best of the Bharata lineage, there was no Kṣatriya equal to him in righteousness. Indeed, the present dharma is the best among all knowers of dharma; the kings anointed him, the great king of kings. Free from grief and fear, awakened from pleasant dreams, the earth's rulers approached the protector of the Bhārata. In the presence of Rāṇṭanu, the chief of the hidden, the kings then followed the rules, and the external affairs turned favorable. Brahman surrounded the Kṣatriya, and the Kṣatriyas followed the vow; those devoted to Brahman and Kṣatriya. He, residing in the beautiful Hastinapura, by breaking the shell of the Kurus, made the earth flow like the ocean. He, resembling the king of the gods, knowledgeable in dharma, truthful in speech, endowed with supreme asceticism and charity. Free from attachment and hatred, with a face like the moon, equal in speed to the wind. Like the earth in patience, he is the embodiment of death. The killing of animals and birds is indeed a fact; Rāṇṭanu, the king, does not engage in this in vain.

386 Possible Purity In the northern region of Dharmabāhya, the self-controlled Śantanu ruled equally over all beings, free from desire. From that time, the rituals began for the deities and the ancestors. And by unrighteousness, no one among the wise was harmed. For the unhappy and the helpless in the lower forms of life, He alone became the father of all beings, the king. In that Kurupatishrī, with the king of kings present, Bharata spoke the truth with a steadfast mind. There were sixteen and eighty-four years, and also others, He became a forest dweller, having attained pleasure in women. Thus, of such form, such conduct, such behavior, and such fall, Gāṅgeya had a son named Devavrata, the Vasu. Skilled in all branches, he was also among the other kings, A great warrior, a great soul, a great charioteer. Once, having spotted a deer, following it along the river, Śantanu, the king, was pleased with the shallow waters of the Bhāgirathī. He thought, "What is this, O best of men? Is this river not flowing as it did before?" Then, desiring a cause, that great-minded one, Saw a young man, endowed with beauty, large, and with charming features. A divine weapon, like that of the god Indra, was prepared.

Page 399

Peel, having surrounded the sharp arrows, stood firm. The king, astonished, saw the two rivers, Ganges nearby, enveloped by those arrows. Having just been born, that son of Śantanu was not remembered by the wise one to recognize his son. He then enchanted his father with illusion, and quickly disappeared from there. At that time, the king Śantanu saw something wonderful there. The Ganges, bearing a splendid form, took the boy in her right hand, adorned him. Adorned with the finest ornaments, she, though previously seen, was not recognized by Śantanu. He said: "You, O king, who were born as my eighth son, this tiger among men, take him to the house." He who is endowed with strength, versed in the Vedas and the branches, is like the king of the gods in battle. What is taught in the scriptures, that is the essence of the gods and demons, O Bhārata.

Page 400

The son of Aṅgiras, honored by gods and demons, What is known in the scriptures is also established here. In you, O mighty-armed one, with great soul, The sage, invincible to others, is Jāmadagni, the valorous. What weapon is known in the Vedas, that is also established here. This great archer, knowledgeable in the ways of kings, I have given my own son, the hero, to you. Then, having obtained permission, Śantanu, With his son shining like the sun, went to his own city. Paurava, having gone to his city, resembling Indra's city, Thought of himself as fulfilled in all desires. Then, in Paurava, he anointed his son for the kingdom. The son of Paurava, the son of Śantanu, with great fame, Also delighted his father with his deeds, O Bharata. Thus, the king, enjoying with his son, Spent four rainy seasons with great valor. At one time, having gone to the forest, near the river Yamunā, The king, unseen, smelled the excellent fragrance. He wandered around, seeking the source of the fragrance.

Page 401

He then bit the maiden, who was in the form of a goddess. He asked her, "O maiden with dark eyes, who are you and what do you desire?" "I am the daughter of the great king Dāsa, by the order of my father." Seeing her, King Śantanu desired the goddess-like maiden. He went to her father and asked for her hand, inquiring about her lineage. The king replied to Dāsa, "A daughter has just been born to me, to be given in marriage." "O Lord of men, if you desire this maiden from me, then listen." "If you wish for her, grant me a promise in truth." "At the right time, I will give you this maiden, for there is no one like you." Rāntanu spoke: "Having heard your promise, I will either give or not give." "If it is to be given, I will give her; otherwise, she shall not be yours." The wives spoke.

Page 402

The son born to you will be the king of the earth; no one else should be anointed as king. Veraṁpāyana said: He did not wish to give that boon to Dāśa. Though burned by the intense grief, he was still in thought, O Bhārata. Then, the king, thinking of the Dāśa maiden, approached Hastinapura, afflicted by sorrow. At that time, seeing Śantanu in meditation, his son Devavrata approached and spoke to his father. "Your welfare should be ensured by your own sons; why then do you grieve here constantly, O distressed one?" "Why do you not speak anything, O king?" Thus addressed by his son, Śantanu replied: "Undoubtedly, I am absorbed in meditation, as I am, so I am." "Only you are our offspring, O great Bhārata; I lament the impermanence of life." "Somehow, O Gāṅgeya, there is no calamity for us." "Undoubtedly, you alone are the best son, even better than a boon." "And I do not wish to marry again; I desire well-being for the continuation of progeny."

Page 403

Unmarried, they say, is the state of having no sons. The three Vedas and sacrifices are not harmed by the absence of offspring. Thus, among men, there is no doubt that where there is a wise son, there is no uncertainty for me. This is the eternal essence of the three ancient texts. You are a hero, always steadfast, O Bhārata; there is no other death for you, O sinless one. I am in distress, how can there be peace for you? Thus, the cause of sorrow has been stated by you in anger. Then, knowing that cause, the great-minded Devavrata, contemplating, went forth. He approached the aged minister, the father’s well-wisher, and asked him about the cause of the father’s sorrow. To him, the chief of the Kurus, he inquired appropriately. Then, Devavrata, accompanied by Kshatriyas, approached the king of the Dasa and sought the daughter himself. The daughters received him, duly honored according to custom, in the royal assembly, O Bhārata.

Page 404

You are indeed the lord, O Tan, of the Jhaṭṭana. What shall I say to you, the best among sons? Who can desire the connection with the desired womb? Even the hundred sacrifices cannot surpass it. The offspring of Chaitadārya, who is equal to you in qualities, Whose son is the glorious Satyavatī. Thus, my father is greatly honored by you. Satyavatī, among all kings of Bharata. Even the sage Aśita was previously rejected by me, For Satyavatī was indeed the best among sages. Due to the girl’s fatherhood, I shall speak, O best of Bharata. I only point out the fault in the strong and capable. For if you are capable, whether of Gandharvas or Asuras, You will never find happiness, O Ma HSE, O Parantapa. This is indeed the only fault, no other king is like this. Know this, O blessed one, regarding the giving and receiving. Thus addressed, O Ma, he replied, To the listening kings, the father of Bharata. This is my opinion, take it, the truth of Satyavatī. Neither born nor born of a horse, I do not dare to speak. Thus is the possibility of birth.

Page 405

Thus, I will do as you speak. The one who is born here will not be a king for us. Having spoken thus, the Dasā replied again, wishing to accomplish the difficult task for the sake of the kingdom, O best of Bharata. Knowing this, the king, devoted to truth and dharma, then acknowledged the words of his father. The kingdom was indeed given up by me earlier, O king of men, and I will also make this decision for the sake of offspring. From today onwards, O Dasā, I shall observe celibacy. Even without a son, my worlds will be eternal in heaven.

Then, the wise Śāntanu was born to Satyavatī. By his valor, a hero named Citrāṅgada was born, who was known among men. Then, the lord again generated the great archer, the king Vichitravīrya, from Satyavatī. The king Śāntanu, who had not attained youth, approached the time of death. In heaven, Śāntanu established Bhīṣma and Citrāṅgada in the kingdom, as per the wishes of Satyavatī. But Citrāṅgada, the valiant one, did not consider any man equal to himself. As he was being thrown, the gods and also the asuras were equal in strength. Thus, a great war arose for him in Kurukṣetra. There, on the banks of the river Hirṇavatī, there was a battle between the two powerful ones. In that clash, amidst the shower of arrows, the mighty Gandharva killed the best of the Kurus. Having made an end of Citrāṅgada, the Gandharva ascended to heaven. In that, the husband of Sādhu was struck down, with great renown.

Bhīṣma, the son of Śantanu, had many daughters. When the names of the kings were being praised, Bhīṣma himself, the lord, then chose those daughters, O king. He spoke to the kings, the sound of his voice like thunder. Bhīṣma, the best of men, took the daughters in his chariot. Some offered a daughter along with a pair of cows. Others, with the wealth mentioned, offered various things. Some, in a state of excitement, approached, while others found their own. The eighth was the wedding, as remembered by the poets. The royal ladies approached the svayaṃvara, seeking to be chosen. They were said to be taken by the dharma speakers. These earth protectors, I wish to conquer by force. You should strive with all your might for victory or otherwise. I stand here, the protector of the earth, determined for battle. Thus speaking, the powerful king Bhīṣma took the daughters in his chariot. He quickly took those daughters. Then all the kings rose up, agitated.

Page 410

The warriors, touching their own weapons, quickly released their arrows. The confusion became immense as they raised their bows. Like the shower of stars, it was for Janamejaya. With the ornaments and the auspicious ones, they shone. They adorned themselves with various armaments. The charioteers, having taken their positions, were ready with their weapons. As they advanced, they prepared for battle. Then, a great war arose between them and that of Bhārata. They hurled thousands of arrows simultaneously. And Bhīṣma swiftly cut down those who had not yet reached. Then all the kings surrounded from all sides. They rained arrows like a shower upon the mountains. That arrow-rain was everywhere. Then, the kings, having taken their positions, prepared for battle. They honored their own charioteers. The warriors also worshipped their own weapons. Thus, the battle commenced.

Page 411

Having defeated the entire army in battle, the skilled warrior set out towards Dhārta, accompanied by maidens. Then, from behind, the great charioteer Śālva, with an immeasurable soul, attacked Bhīṣma, the son of Śantanu, in battle. As he struck the elephant in the thigh, the powerful leader of the troops, having received the signal, advanced. The king said to Bhīṣma, "Stand firm, stand firm!" Then, the mighty Śālva, urged by the enraged, powerful Bhīṣma, advanced. Then, that man-lion Bhīṣma, unperturbed by the great army, stood firm like a cloud ablaze with anger. Having taken his position in the battle, free from fear and confusion, the great charioteer advanced towards Śālva. Seeing him retreating, the kings were all astonished and exclaimed, "Well done, well done!" The warriors, like bulls, roared in the midst of the battle, engaging one another with great valor. Then, the best of men, Bhīṣma, was showered with thousands of arrows by the great king Śālva. The kings, having seen Bhīṣma previously attacked by Śālva, were astonished and exclaimed, "Well done, well done!" The warriors observed his lightness in battle.

Thus addressed, the virtuous one replied: "This is indeed the answer to your question." Without doubt, the supreme dharma is known to you. One should abandon the earth, the fragrance, and the essence of water; one should also abandon light, form, and the qualities of air. One should give up the brilliance of the comet and the warmth; one should abandon sound and the coolness of the moon. Then, the renowned ṛṣi Utaṭhya was indeed wise. Mamatā was his wife, known for her supreme respect. Among the foremost of the Yavīyas of Utaṭhya, the great brilliance of Bṛhaspati approached Mamatā. 408 Saṃbhavapava To produce sons, O blind one, the great sage spoke thus. Thus addressed, the radiant one replied, "So be it." The king then sent his wife, Sudeṣṇā. It is not possible to remain unspoken about such matters. You are indeed our truth, you are the ultimate refuge; therefore, having heard my words, do as you deem fit thereafter. Page 422

Today I went to the river Yamunā. He, having approached the Yamunā, came to me then, the foremost sage, afflicted by desire, spoke sweetly. Having overwhelmed me, the great one took me, a child, with his brilliance, and enveloped the world in darkness, O Bhārata. The fish-scented one was previously repulsive to me; the sage gave me this auspicious fragrance. Parāśarya, the great yogi, became a great sage; he was previously known as Dvaipāyana, the son of a maiden. He who, through austerity, spread the Vedas, is the Lord sage; in the world, he attained the name Vyāsa and also the identity of Kṛṣṇa. The truthful one, self-controlled, an ascetic, having burned away sins, was appointed by me and you, O one of immense radiance. He will generate auspicious offspring in the fields of his brother. Indeed, he spoke to me there, "Remember me in your actions." I will remember him, O mighty-armed one, if you, Bhīṣma, wish it. The pure one is born.

Page 423

Your son, O Anumata, will surely produce sons in the fields of Vichitravirya. The great sage Kīṭa spoke to Bhīma, the wise one. Desire and attachment are threefold, and they are distinct from one another. He who contemplates wisely and decides rightly is the wise one. This is indeed the righteous and beneficial path that you have spoken of, which pleases me greatly. Then, having made this promise, Bhīma, O scion of the Kurus, began to think of the sage. He, knowing the Vedas, contemplated what was known to the mother. In an instant, he appeared, known to the scion of the Kurus. At that time, he performed worship for the son, the one worthy of reverence. Embracing him with both arms, he anointed him with the sacred water, and he shed tears for his son. Having performed the anointing, the great sage, having saluted, spoke to his mother, the son of Puṅja, Vyāsa. Whatever you desire, I shall fulfill that, O BAPTA. Teach me, O knower of dharma, what is dear to you.

Page 425

The one who knows Dharma, truth, and the supreme beyond, and the one who does not know Dharma, as your mind is directed towards Dharma. Therefore, I will fulfill your desire, as it is ancient. I will give sons to my brother, equal to the friends of Varuṇa, as you have instructed me here. For a year, according to justice, it will be pure thereafter; no maiden who is unchaste will approach me. Satyavatī said: As soon as the goddess becomes pregnant, do so; in lawless kingdoms, there are no rain-giving deities. How can a kingdom be sustained, O Lord? Therefore, Bhīṣma will kill that one. Vyāsa said: If a son is to be given by me quickly, may this deformity be borne by her and also that of the other. If I can bear the fragrance, form, appearance, and body, let the goddess Kausalyā become pregnant. Vairāmpāyana said:

Page 427

Kausalyā, the deity is present today, and you will enter. The attentive one will arrive by waiting. Hearing that speech, the lying ones in the auspicious bed thought then of Bhīṣma and the other Kurupāṇḍavas. Then, in Ambikā, the truthful sage was first appointed. He entered the bed where the lamps were glowing. From Krishna's shining matted hair, the eyebrows and these eyes closed, O goddess. That night was born from her mother's dear wish. Out of fear, the daughter of Kāśi could not look at him due to anger. Then, having approached, the mother spoke to her son: "Even in this, the son will be virtuous; he will be a royal son." Hearing the words of the mother, the son, very wise, spoke, prompted by the divine knowledge. He who has the life of a thousand serpents, the best among the learned, will be very fortunate, very mighty, and will have great wisdom. And also, his sons will be very strong. However, due to the mother's bad qualities, he will be blind. Hearing that speech, the mother then addressed her son: "The king of the Kurus will not be blind, he will be a fitting son." The protector of the family lineage, the enhancer of the lineage of the fathers.

Page 428

416 Possible Purity You are worthy to give birth to the second king of the Kuru dynasty. Thus, having made that promise, the great ascetic departed. Kausalyā also gave birth to a blind son. Again, that goddess, having spoken, called the sage, just as before, the blameless one. By that very means, the great fortune came to her. Ambālikā also approached him, or she too was there. The Pandavas, resembling the color of the sky, became distressed. Seeing her frightened, resembling the Pandavas, and sorrowful, the son of Partha, Vyāsa, the son of Satyavatī, spoke this word. Since you have attained the pallor of the Pandavas, seeing me also deformed, this son of yours will indeed be pale. And his name will also be the same here, having a beautiful face. Thus speaking, the great sage departed. Then, having calmed down, Satyā spoke to her son. She praised the pallor of that child. The mother then requested another son. The great sage replied to her, "So be it." Then the goddess gave birth to a son, endowed with the characteristics of Pandu, shining like wealth.

Page 429

The five Pāṇḍavas were born to him. At the time of the season, he arranged for the elder's marriage. She, contemplating the form and fragrance, did not heed the words of the goddess out of fear, resembling the son of the gods. Then, having adorned her with her own ornaments, she became like an Apsara for Kṛṣṇa, the son of Karipati. The maid, having approached the sage, bowed down and, having received permission, entered, having honored him. By indulging in sensual pleasures, he was pleased with her. "Arise, you will not be defeated; this is your auspicious path, the illustrious one has arrived." Then, he will be the best among all wise men. Vidura was born, the son of Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, the brother of Dhṛtarāṣṭra and the wise Pāṇḍu. Dharma, in the form of Vidura, free from the curse of that great soul, was devoid of desire and anger. Having become free from debt to Dharma, he again entered the womb, having gathered there. Thus, the son of Dvaipāyana, of such diverse valor.

Page 431

I asked the king about the situation of the ascetic. By which path did the robbers, the best of the twice-born, go? Let us go quickly, then. Thus, while the ascetic was speaking, the king did not fear, saying, "Do not be afraid, O king." Then the royal men searched for that hermitage and saw there the ascetic and the wealth of the moon. Then, the protector of the ascetics approached that sage and, having controlled himself, reported to the king. The king, along with the robbers, was advised to be cautious. The great ascetic, knowing of the impending danger, was pierced by a spear. Then, having raised the spear, the protectors of the sage returned to the king, taking away wealth as they pleased. Though he was starving, the sage did not succumb to death. He began to breathe and brought the sages along. While the spear was being heated by the great ascetic, the sages experienced great suffering. They, having become birds at night, surrounded him and asked the best of the ascetics as much as they could.

Page 432

420 Saṃbhavapava We wish to hear what sin you have committed. Then that sage's messenger spoke to the ascetics: "Who shall I go to for blame? No one else has offended me." Hearing this, the king, along with his ministers, pleased the sage, the best of ascetics, who was pierced by a spear. "Due to my delusion and ignorance, I have caused this, I offer my respects to you; you should not be angry with me." Thus addressed by the king, the sage granted his favor. The king, having received his favor, then descended. And as he descended, he saw the spear; he took the spear and broke it. Thus, the sage wandered with that spear, and through his penance, he conquered the worlds. It is said in the world, "Aṇīmaṇḍavya." Going to the abode of the brāhmaṇa, the supreme knower of dharma, he found the lord seated there, firmly established in dharma. What sin have I committed unknowingly, for which this result has come to me? Quickly tell me the truth; I am the strength of my penance.

Page 434

The Kurus flourished in Kurukṣetra. The land became fruitful after the destruction, yielding crops and fruits. The vehicles were joyful, and the animals and birds were delighted. The merchants and artisans thrived in the cities, and the archers and learned men were happy. Some people, devoid of unrighteousness, were not troubled even in the regions of the kingdoms. Those engaged in charitable acts and devoted to sacrifices, united in mutual affection, protected the populace. People free from pride and anger, avoiding greed, supported each other. That great and prosperous city shone like a mountain adorned with clouds. Resembling the city of Indra, it was surrounded by palatial structures. In the rivers, forested areas, and beautiful gardens, the joyful people thrived. The Kurus to the north and the Kuravas to the south were in conflict, moving about in the pursuit of success. The pure and sacred emerged.

Page 435

The wretched do not become prosperous, how can the widows? In that delightful city, many were created by the Kurus; there were wells, gardens, and Brahmin residences as well. Under the rule of Bhīṣma, the king was protected from all sides. It became beautiful, marked by the sacrificial post, and this land was adorned by foreign nations. The wheel of dharma was established by Bhīṣma. While activities were being performed, all the great souls among the princes became constantly festive. In the houses of the chief Kurus and the citizens, the words "Let it be given, let it be enjoyed" were heard. Pāṇḍu and Vidura, the wise one, were protected by Bhīṣma from birth like sons. They were cultured by rituals, engaged in vows and studies, and they reached youth through labor and exercise. In archery, wrestling, mace fighting, and swordplay, as well as in the art of elephant management, they excelled. In the histories and Puranas, and in various teachings, those knowledgeable in the Vedas and their limbs were diligent everywhere. In archery, Pāṇḍu was superior to other men.

Page 437

Thus, it should be arranged by me and you, without enmity. It is heard that there is a suitable daughter of Subala's family, as well as the daughter of the king of Madra. The beautiful and the noble ones are indeed related to us, the Kṣatriyas. I think they should be chosen, as I consider the progeny of this lineage, or what Vidura thinks. Vidura said: You are the father, you are the mother, you are our supreme teacher. Therefore, consider what is beneficial for this lineage. Then, Śuka heard from the brāhmaṇas about Gāndhārī, the daughter of Subala, who worshipped the boon-giving god, the remover of the eyes of the world. Gāndhārī indeed has a hundred sons, which is auspicious. Thus, having heard this truly, Bhīṣma, the grandfather of the Kurus, then sent a message to the king of Gāndhāra. There, the consideration of Subala was not hidden; he observed both fame and conduct with wisdom. He sent Gāndhārī, the virtuous one, to Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

Page 439

Amya, having vowed to his own son, the valiant one. He gave the maiden, named Agrajātā, to Kuntibhoja, his friend, for the sake of friendship. She was appointed in her father's house for the worship of the divine guests. With great determination, he performed the fierce external vow. He, with a hidden resolve, who was known as Duvāsasa, pleased the fierce one, who was self-controlled. He gave her, observing the auspicious signs, to the sage, who was afflicted by curses. Whichever deity you invoke with this mantra, by their grace, your son will come to be. Thus spoken by the Brahmin, she, out of curiosity, the virtuous maiden, invoked the glorious deity. She saw the sun, the sustainer of the worlds, approaching, and was astonished at that great wonder. Illuminating, the sun then placed a seed in her womb, and from that, a hero was born, renowned among the warriors. The illustrious one, adorned with the divine armor, was enveloped in glory. Holding the natural armor, with a face radiant like a jewel, a son was born, renowned in all worlds.

Page 440

The girl was given to him again, he being the supreme one. Then, the best among givers ascended to heaven. Fearing the relatives, Kuntī secretly placed the well-formed son in the water. Then, the embryo was released, and the greatly famous one, Rādhābhata, was conceived as a son. The name of that child was to be given as he was born with Vasu, "May he be Vasuṣeṇa." While being killed, the strong one, endowed with weapons, approached the sun from behind. In that moment, the hero, who was chanting the truth, was not present in the external sounds of that great soul. Indra, becoming a Brahmin, for the sake of alms, the one who creates beings, sought the armor with great radiance. Taking the blood-dripping armor from his own body, Karṇa, cutting the earring, offered it with folded hands. He gave that to him, astonished, who spoke the words of the gods, demons, humans, Gandharvas, serpents, and Rakṣasas. In that, you will be thrown, and by this, it will not happen. Long ago, it was heard that his name was Suṣeṇa. Then, by the action of Vaikatanā, he became so. The pure one is born.

Page 441

Vērāmpāyana said: Endowed with beauty, essence, and qualities, the great vow of Dharmarāja, the daughter of Kuntibhoja, was for the sake of her father in the svayaṃvara. In the midst of thousands of earth's heroes, he found Pāṇḍu, who had the lion's teeth, elephant's shoulders, and bull's eyes, and was very strong. With her, the son of Kuntibhoja, the Kurunandana, united, blessed with immense fortune, like Pulomā with Maghavan. Even with Devavrata, the daughter of Madra, renowned in the three worlds, was known as Mādri, the daughter of the king of Madra. Renowned among all kings for her beauty, she was sought after for the sake of Pāṇḍu with great wealth at that time. Bhīṣma arranged the marriage of the great-souled Pāṇḍu. The strong Pāṇḍu, with lion-like shoulders, elephant's shoulders, and bull's eyes, was admired by men on earth. Endowed with the strength of Pāṇḍu, he, desiring to conquer the earth, faced many enemies.

Page 442

The warriors who were previously defeated in battle by the lion among men, Pāṇḍu, are now honored. The enemies of the valiant ones, who are the protectors of the earth, were defeated by the one who was killed in Rajagṛha. Then, taking the Kauravas, the vehicles and forces, Pāṇḍu went to Mithilā and defeated the Videhas in battle. Thus, in the battle of the Kauravas, with the strength of his own arms, he made the Bharata shine. The great flame of arrows, like the fire of Pāṇḍu, consumed the kings. They, with their armies, were destroyed by the powerful kings. All were defeated on the earth, all the rulers of the earth. They regarded him as the hero among the gods, like Purandara. All the kings bowed down with folded hands. They approached the wealth, taking various riches. Jewels, pearls, gold, silver, and various chariots and elephants.

Page 443

The king of Nagpur seized that. Pandava, having taken that, again with the chariot of joy, will delight the kingdoms and the city with the help of elephants. The sound of the lost Kītija was revived by Pandu, of the wise Sāntanu and Bharata. Those who previously destroyed the Kauravas were made to pay by the lion of Nagpur, Pandu. Thus spoke the kings and the ministers gathered, their minds pleased, along with the townspeople and the subjects. They, having approached him, advanced like rivers, led by Bhīṣma. She was being poured out like a stream then. Of the separate streams of the unblemished essence. A hundred and one full, as appropriate, O lord of the worlds. This one will also be a king after him. Indeed, this is my truth that will surely happen here. In the death of this statement, O Bharata, The flesh-eating, fierce beings and the terrible ones will arise. Having observed the ominous signs, they are terrible. The pure one will arise.

Vidura, the great sage, said: Your son will certainly be the one who brings about the end of your lineage. His peace will be great in abandoning the auspicious one. Let there be one less than a hundred sons for you, O king. With one, the Kuru will ensure the welfare of the world and the family. One should abandon for the sake of the family, and for the sake of the village, one should abandon the family. For the sake of the village, one should abandon the earth for one's own benefit. Thus, Vidura spoke, and all those who were wise agreed. But the king, attached to his sons, did not act accordingly. Then, within a month, a hundred sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra were born, and one daughter named Jātāghika was born. In Gandhāra, she was nurtured with care. Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the great king, was indeed blessed. In that year, the king Dhṛtarāṣṭra, of great fame, had a son named Yuyutsu, who was wise. Thus, a hundred sons were born to the wise Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and among the great warriors, there was one daughter named Duḥśalā.

काममन्युपरीतापि FAAS च | Noble men, engrossed in sinful desires, reject the cruel ones. न विधिं ग्रसते प्रज्ञा प्रज्ञां तु ग्रसते विधिः | Wisdom does not consume the law; rather, the law consumes wisdom. WIAA मुख्ये कुठे जातस्य भारत | O India, where is the mind of one overwhelmed by lust? पाण्ड्रुवाच | Bhīma said: शत्रूणां या वधे वृत्तिः सा मृगाणां वधे स्मृता | The conduct regarding the killing of enemies is remembered in the killing of animals. राज्ञां मृग न मां मोहात्त्व॑ गर्हयितुमर्हसि | O king, do not blame me out of delusion. अच्छद्मनामायया च मृगाणां वध इष्यते | The killing of animals is desired by the one named Achchhadma. स एव धर्मो राज्ञां तु तद्वदवान्किं नु गहसे | That is indeed the dharma of kings; why do you not understand this? अगस्त्यः सत्रमासीनश्चचार मृगयामृषिः | Agastya, seated in the assembly, roamed in the hunt. BYR possible wind Whether heedless or not, the one who is open is grasped by strength. Why do you hunt the deer? The deer said: I do not hunt, O king of deer, due to self-causation. But I shall wait for union with you. In the time of well-being for all beings, who indeed would kill a deer wandering in the forest? What you have done is false regarding the beloved of human effort. Among the sages of the Poravāṇas, who is there to match this? The great sin of human beings is condemned in the world. It is unworthy and unrighteous, O Bhārata. The one who knows the essence of scriptures and the nature of truth, is not like the divine, who is not to be harmed. By you, the sinful actions of humans are to be restrained, O best of kings, who are to be avoided by the three worlds. What have you done, O best of men, that you have caught me unawares? The sage who eats roots and fruits, disguised as a deer, O king. Always residing in the forest, ever devoted to peace. I have been harmed by you, therefore I shall not spare you.

Page 455

The horse, having been enchanted by desire, will surely meet its end. I am indeed a sage named Kindama, unmatched in austerity, who has engaged in the act of mating among men. Having become a deer, I roam in the dense forest with other deer; this act of killing will not lead you to the sin of Brahma-hatya, as you do not know. Having taken the form of a deer, I am thus enchanted by desire. However, you will attain the fruit of this, for you will indeed enjoy companionship with your beloved, enchanted by desire. You too will go to the realm of the dead in this state. At the end, you will go to the beloved with whom you have associated, having reached the great difficulty of all beings. A devotee will follow you, being wise and superior in service. As you have brought me sorrow, so too will happiness come to you, and sorrow will approach you. Thus speaking, the deer, greatly afflicted by sorrow, detached from life, fell down in an instant.

Page 456

The sage spoke: The king, having crossed over the sorrow of his own kin, was afflicted in the assembly. Pāṇḍu said: Even the virtuous, due to their actions, fall into great difficulties, being ensnared by the web of desire. AAAI was born as a child, my father. Having attained the end of life, it is said that he is indeed a being of desire. In that field of the king, the sage, with controlled speech, directly gave birth to me, the divine. Today, my understanding is afflicted by calamity; this is my lowly state, forsaking the divine in the hunt of the wicked. I shall strive for liberation, for bondage is indeed a great affliction; I shall follow the good conduct of my eternal father. I shall connect with the highly ascetic one. Therefore, I alone, in this solitary tree, shall wander, observing the sage in this earth. The pure one is born.

Page 457

The one who is covered by dust, without a place of refuge, residing at the root of a tree, indifferent to all that is dear and undear. Neither grieving nor rejoicing, equal in praise and blame, without hope, without salutation, without attachment, without possessions. Neither mocking anyone, nor desiring a hut, always with a cheerful face, engaged in the welfare of all beings. One who is free from all violence, equal to all living beings, always the same towards those who breathe. At one time, wandering as a beggar, whether in a family or among five, even if unable to obtain food, wandering without eating. Little by little, as much as can be eaten, by the gain of food, never exceeding the limit, in the absence of gain, filling seven. With one arm cut off, with one side adorned with sandalwood, neither auspicious nor inauspicious, meditating on both. Not wishing to live, nor acting like one who wishes to die, neither rejoicing in life nor hating death. Those who are alive, capable of performing actions of upliftment, they are all situated beyond the moment. In those states, abandoning all sense activities, having renounced all, with a pure mind, free from impurities. Freed from all sins, transcending one's own speech.

Page 458

Not spoken by anyone, standing in the midst of the wind. With this continuous conduct, I will destroy fearlessly, having established myself. I do not walk on the path of the dog, unworthy of compassion. One who is engaged in any conduct with a desire for pleasure, he wanders the path of dogs. Veraṁpāyana said: Thus speaking, the king, deeply troubled, with a great sigh, looked at Kuntī and spoke to Mādrī. Kausalyā, Vidura, the king, and the relatives, along with Satyavatī, Bhīṣma, and the royal priest. The great-souled brāhmaṇas, drinkers of soma, and the aged Poruvas, who reside there, are our refuge. All should be spoken to, O Pāṇḍu, in the forest. Hearing the words of the steadfast one in the forest, Kuntī and Mādrī spoke the same words. There are also other hermitages that can be approached, O best of the Bharatas, along with the wives of the hermits, for great austerity. You alone will be the essence, even of heaven. The pure one.

Page 459

In the practice of self-discipline, we shall perform great austerities, abandoning desires and pleasures. If you, O lord of the people, abandon us, we shall surely be defeated here. Pāṇḍu said: If this is determined, I shall follow the path of my father, adhering to the conduct of these two warriors. Abandoning the pleasures of life, I shall engage in great austerities, living on fruits and roots in the great forest. In the forest, both of us shall endure the hardships of hunger and thirst, and the weariness of the body. Enduring the cold, heat, hunger, and thirst, this body shall be worn down by severe austerities. Contemplating in solitude, I shall perform austerities with words dedicated to the ancestors and deities. Even for a hermit, the actions of the householders are not to be neglected; what to speak of those living in the village? Thus, I shall take on a more severe form of austerity in the forest. I shall remain steadfast in this body until its end. Vairāmpāyana said:

Page 460

Thus, the king of the Kaurava lineage spoke. Then he gave the crown jewel and earrings. He also gave the fine garments and ornaments. After giving everything to the brāhmaṇas, Pāṇḍu spoke again. Pāṇḍu, having gone to the city of serpents, entered the forest. Abandoning all desires for wealth, pleasure, and the supreme bliss of the soul, the great Kurupūngava set out. Then, his attendants followed him. Hearing the various cries of compassion from Bharata's lion, they lamented, making a sound like Bhīma's lament. Shedding warm tears, they left the king behind and went to the city of Noga, taking grass with them. Hearing all this, the best of men, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, grieved for Pāṇḍu in the great forest as it happened. The prince, Pāṇḍu, who was eating the fruit of the Pāṇḍu tree, went to the mountain assembly with his wives. Having reached the Chaitraratha, he crossed the river Varuṇa and proceeded to the Himalayas, overcoming great obstacles, heading towards Gandhamādana. Protected by great beings and sages, the king then resided in all kinds of difficulties. Thus, the pure one was born.

Page 462

The gardens of Kubera are similar and uneven; great rivers and mountains are also there. There are eternal snow and trees, and some great beings; some are unapproachable. No bird should transgress, for other animals do; the wind alone passes here, and the perfected ones are supreme. How can these enemies of the mountain king go? May they not suffer, O Bharatarṣabha. Pāṇḍu said: The great one without offspring does not see a wife. With debts combined, humans are born on earth. These, however, as they are, if a person does not understand, he has no worlds; thus, the knowledge of dharma is established. The sacrificer pleases the gods with study and austerity, and also with offerings to the ancestors, showing compassion to humans. I am released from the heat of the sages, gods, and humans; thus, I am liberated from the bondage of ancestors. In the destruction of the body, there is indeed destruction for the ancestors; hence, from here, the best of men are born for the sake of progeny.

Page 463

As I was created by that great soul in my father's lineage, how can my offspring be born in this field of mine? There is indeed a divine and auspicious offspring of yours, O king, born of the sinless lineage, seen with divine vision. A man, wise and ungrasped by the fruits of actions, attains what is destined for him by the gods. In that case, O father, you should not hesitate; you will attain a virtuous offspring that will bring you joy. Hearing this from the ascetic, Pandu became absorbed in thought, knowing the action that had been obstructed. He spoke to Kuntī, the illustrious wife, about the means of producing offspring in times of distress. In the world, offspring is known as the establishment of dharma. Thus, Kuntī, the wise, understood the eternal dharma from the beginning. What is desired, given, performed as penance, and followed by rules, all of it is not considered pure without offspring here. Knowing this, I shall proceed with a pure smile, thinking that I will not attain the auspicious offspring.

Page 464

Due to the curse of the deer, my progeny is lost, indeed, of the unselfish. O coward, just as the slain are thus. These are the relatives and heirs, O team of Dharma, listen to me, O Pṛthī, the six heirs. From the self-sacrifice, the son who is well-formed and the one born of a free woman. The one who is given, the one who is artificial, and the one who approaches himself, and the one who is born of a lowly womb. Considering the absence of the eastern and western, he should seek the son. The best and the worst of men desire a son in calamity. The righteous, who yield the fruit of Dharma, find the offspring. Even from the self-sperm, Manu, the self-born, spoke. You are indeed devoid of progeny, O TASES. You, O illustrious one, are either similar or superior in glory. Listen, Kunti, to this tale regarding Śāradaṇḍāyani, which is about the birth of the hero's offspring, appointed by the teacher. With flowers, having bathed at night, Kunti at the crossroads, having chosen the accomplished brāhmaṇa, having performed the fire sacrifice for progeny. When she was engaged in her duties, she dwelled there with her husband. There, she gave birth to three sons, the great charioteers. The birth is possible.

Page 466

In that sacrifice, the divine beings, including Indra, approached the great soul, the righteous one. Indra, with the moon and the southern winds, performed the sacrifice. The king, Vyūṣitāśva, shone like the sun, surpassing all beings in brilliance. Having conquered and taken hold, the best of kings ruled over the eastern, western, and southern regions. In the great Ashvamedha sacrifice, the powerful Vyūṣitāśva became a king, accompanied by a host of elephants. The ancient scholars sing of Vyūṣitāśva, who conquered the land up to the ocean. He protected all his sons, like a father. The patron of the great sacrifice gave wealth to those outside. He also brought forth many excellent offerings. His sister, Kākṣīvatī, was of supreme beauty, resembling the goddess Bhadrā among men. The two desired each other, as the scriptures say. In that desire, he became afflicted with illness. The pure one was born.

Page 467

He soon went to the setting sun like a wooden figure. In that corpse, the king of men was distressed by grief. The childless man-eater weeps, as we have heard. O king, know that the fortunate one is greatly afflicted. A woman who is knowledgeable in the highest dharma, without a son, lives without her husband; she does not live, she is distressed. Without a husband, I wish to go, O best of Kṣatriyas; please be gracious and lead me. Without you, I cannot bear to live even for a moment. O king, grant me your favor and lead me quickly. I will follow you from behind, through all difficulties. I, O best of men, will go where you go. Like a shadow, I will always speak, O king, remaining devoted to the welfare of the beloved. From today onwards, O king, the painful heart-wrenching will overwhelm me, without you, O lotus-eyed one. Surely, I am unfortunate; I have been separated from my companions. The connections are either due to the wise or the royal bodies. This is the result of sinful actions accumulated in previous bodies. The suffering that has come to me, O king, is due to your absence.

Page 469

Thus addressed by her, the king spoke again to that goddess: "This is indeed the best speech, free from faults." Thus, Kunti, having been addressed, performed a great deed, As you have said, O auspicious one, he indeed became like an immortal. Now I shall speak of this, so listen to me: The ancient sages have described this as the supreme truth. In ancient times, the beautiful ones were unrestrained, Enjoying pleasures, free with their eyes like horses. Among those wandering maidens, the fortunate ones had husbands; There was no sin in their union, for he was indeed of ancient lineage. And those who were born of the people of the city, Even today, they are still engaged in desires, avoiding faults. This dharma, as seen in the ancient texts, is revered by the great sages. In the northern regions, the fame of Ramba still exists; He is indeed the eternal dharma, a benefactor of friends. In this world, soon, by me, this pure smile was established; From which it is to be elaborated. There was a sage named Udalaka, as we have heard; His son was known as Śvetaketu, who became a sage. This was done by me, as we have heard, for human beings; O lotus-eyed one, understand the reason for this.

Page 470

458 Saṃbhavapava Once, the Brahmin caught the hand of Śvetaketu in front of his mother and father, saying, "Let us go." The son of the sage, having become angry, forcibly took his mother away. The father, seeing him angry, spoke to Śvetaketu, "Do not be angry, my son; this is the eternal dharma." Indeed, all beings are not covered by the same qualities; just as cows stand in their respective colors, so do the progeny. The son of the sage then looked at Śvetaketu and spoke to him, "This is a sin for men of great fortune; it is not so for other beings." From that time onward, it has been heard that this is how it will be. The sin of killing a fetus will bring unhappiness from today onward. Regarding the unmarried girl, the celibate, when the unmarried girl is separated, it will indeed become a sin for the devoted wife. The one appointed by her husband and the one who is a devoted wife will not do this; this will indeed happen. Thus, the fearful one was established by the son of Uddālaka, Śvetaketu.

सोदासेन च रम्भोरु नियुक्तापत्यजन्मनि | Madayantī went to Vasiṣṭha, as we have heard. CTSA च सा FARRAH नाम भामिनी | The lady named Farrah is from CTSA. अस्माकमपि ते जन्म विदितं कमलेक्षणे | Your birth is also known, O lotus-eyed one. अत एतानि सर्वाणि कारणानि समीक्ष्य वे | Considering all these reasons, ऋतावृतौ राजपुत्रि सरिया भरता यतव्रते | In the season of righteousness, O princess, रोषेष्वन्येषु कालेषु Caldas स्त्री किलाहंति | In other times of anger, the Caldas woman indeed speaks. भता भायां राजपुत्रि धर्म्यं वाधम्यमेव वा | O princess, whether it is righteous or not, विोषतः पुत्रगृद्धी हीनः प्रजननात्स्वयम् | The son, devoid of desire, is born from his own. तथा रक्ताङ्गुलितलः पद्मपत्रनिभः शुभे | Thus, with red fingers, like a lotus petal, प्रसादार्थं मया तेऽयं रिरस्यभ्युदयतोऽञ्जलिः | For your grace, I offer this with uplifted hands. मन्नियोगात्सुकेशान्ते द्विजातेस्तपसाधिकात् | By my command, you should produce the leaves, त्वत्कृतेऽहं पृथुश्रोणि गच्छेयं पुत्रिणां गतिम् | For you, I shall go to the path of the sons.

Yeo possible purity Thus addressed, Kuntī spoke to Pāṇḍu, the conqueror of enemies: "Speak, O noble one, who is devoted to the welfare of the beloved." "I, a girl, am engaged in the worship of the invited guests, there I serve the fierce one, the external vow." "Concealing the determination, they know that it is a difficult task; I, having a determined mind, did not please him." "That which is mixed with malice, the Lord revealed the boon; He granted me the mantra, just as the ocean does to me." "Whichever deity you will summon with this mantra, whether desiring or not desiring, that is yours." Thus I spoke then to the father of the Vedas, O Bharata; the Brahmin spoke the truth, and this Kato has arrived." "By your permission, O deity, I shall summon; by this mantra, O king, may our subjects be prosperous." "I summon which deity, tell me, O best among the wise; I am waiting for your instruction, standing in this place." Pāṇḍu spoke: "Today, O noble one, strive according to the proper method; surely summon the auspicious one, for he is indeed a share of the virtuous among the gods." "By unrighteousness, our righteousness shall not be joined at any time."

Page 474

In the middle of the day, the sun is worshipped on the auspicious date. Kuntī, endowed with prosperity and fame, gave birth to a son at the right time. There is no doubt that he will be the greatest among the righteous. He is known as Yudhiṣṭhira, the first son of Pāṇḍu. He will become a renowned king, famous in the three worlds, endowed with strength and virtuous conduct. Having obtained that son, Pāṇḍu then spoke again: "Choose a son who is the strongest among the Kṣatriyas." Then, as spoken by her husband, she invoked the wind, and from that, the mighty Bhīma, the one of great prowess, was born. He was born as a powerful child, and it was declared that he is the greatest among all the strong. This was indeed wonderful; as soon as he was born, he shook the earth, causing the stone to crumble. Kuntī, having given birth, was astonished and did not realize that her son was born. Then, the son, like a thunderbolt, fell to the ground, and with that fall, his body was shattered into a hundred pieces. That stone, which was shattered, was a source of wonder to Pāṇḍu.

Page 475

In that very day, Bhīma was born, the best of the Bharatas. Even the wealth of Duyodhana was born there, the lord of the earth. When born in the womb of Vrikodara, Pāṇḍu thought again, "How can my son, the best among men, become the greatest?" In the deeds of the gods and men, this indeed is established. There, the god is enjoyed according to the prescribed time. Indra is indeed the king of the gods, as we have heard. He is immeasurably strong and powerful, with great might. Having pleased him with austerity, I shall obtain a mighty son, Whom I shall give, he will be my son, he will be the most excellent. By action, mind, and speech, therefore, I shall perform great austerity. Then, the great-splendor Pāṇḍu, having taken me in his mind, Ordered Kuntī to perform a virtuous vow for a year. And with his own self, the mighty-armed one stood on one leg, Performing fierce austerity with supreme meditation. He worshipped the lord of the three worlds. Sūrya, with his own self, approached Bhārata. Then, with a great voice, Vāsava spoke, "I shall give you a son renowned in the three worlds." To the gods, the brāhmaṇas, and friends, the achievers, I shall give you a son, the best, who will destroy all enemies.

Page 476

The king Kaurava, thus addressed by the great Vasava, spoke to Kuntī, remembering the words of the lord of the gods. The great-souled one, endowed with wisdom like the sun, is difficult to overcome and possesses a remarkable intellect. O virtuous one, give birth to a son of noble lineage, endowed with the valor of kṣatriyas, having received the grace of the lord of the gods, the pure-smiling one. Thus addressed, the illustrious Kuntī called upon Śakra, and then the lord of the gods arrived to grant her a boon. As soon as the boy was born, the voice of the incorporeal one resounded in the sky with a great and profound sound. Kuntī, equal to the Kātavīrya in valor, will give birth to one who will be renowned like Śakra. As the affection of Aditi for Viṣṇu was established, so will Arjuna increase the affection for you. This one, having made an alliance with the Madras and the Kauravas, will bring prosperity to the Kuru lineage. By the strength of his arms, the one who carries the sacrificial offerings will satisfy all beings with his abundance. Having conquered the kings of the earth, the mighty one, along with his brothers, will seize the wealth of the brave.

Page 477

Jāmadagnya is equal to Kunti, equal to Vishnu in valor; he will be the greatest among the valorous in the future. Thus, he will bring forth all divine and celestial weapons; he will restore the lost glory of the Brahmins. Kunti's son spoke this wondrous speech, which the wind carried to the sky, and Kunti heard it. Hearing the speech of the ascetics, there arose great joy among the hosts of the hundred. Thus, there was a tumultuous sound of drums from the divine sages and the celestial beings. A great uproar arose, covered by a shower of flowers, as the assembled gods honored Partha. The Kadraveyas, the Vainateyas, and the Gandharvas, along with all the great sages, Bharadvāja, Kaśyapa, Gautama; Viśvāmitra, and Jamadagni; and he who was invoked by the sun, he too came here. Marīci, Aṅgiras, and Pulastya, Dakṣa, the Prajāpati, and the Gandharvas. The Apsarās, adorned with divine garlands and garments, sang and danced around Bhīma. Accompanied by the illustrious Tumburu, he sang.

Bhīma, Ograsen, and Uṛṇāyura, the sinless one; TOE GAIA, seventh; | 044 | Simultaneously, Tṛṇapa, Kaśinanda, and Chitraratha; Thirteenth, Śālirirā; WIAA, fourteenth; | 045 | Kali, Pañcadaṅśa, and also Nārada, the sixteenth; Sadvā, Bṛhadvā, Bṛhaka, Karāṭa, and Mahāyaśa; | 076 | Brahmacārī, endowed with many virtues, and Supaṇa, renowned; Viśvāvasu, Bhauma, and Suchandra, the tenth; | 047 | Mītamādhuyam, endowed with wealth, and the famous Gītamādhuyam; Thus, these divine Gandharvas sang there; ALAA | 048 | Similarly, the Apsarases, adorned with ornaments; NY bn The great fortunate Nṛtū, and they danced, even in the sky; | 059 | Anūnā, and Anavadyā, the beloved, and the best of qualities; Adrīkā, and also Sācī, Miśrakeśī, and Alambusā; | 050 | Marīci, Śiśukā, and also Vidyutpaṇā, Tilottamā; Agrikā, Lakṣaṇā, Kṣemā, Devī, Rambhā, and Manoramā; | 051 | Asitā, and Subāhu, and also Supriyā, and Suvapu; Puṇḍarīkā, Sugandhā, and Surathā, the one who causes disturbance; | 052 | Kāmya, Śāradatī, and they danced there, in groups; Menakā, Sahajanyā, and Pārṇikā, Puñjikasthalā; | 053 | Saṃbhavapava.

The place of the sacrifice is the ghee, and the universe is also the offering. It is known as Uṣo, and it is called FSA and SAT. The killer, the friend, Varuṇa, Bhagā, Indra, Vivasvān, Pūṣa, Tvaṣṭa, and Savitā. Pajān̄jaya and also Viṣṇu, the Adityas, and the fire, standing in the sky, are killing the glory of the Pāṇḍava. The deer hunter and Bae, the great ones, Ajekapāda, Hivundhya, and Pinākī, the scorcher. Dahana, the lord, and Kapālī, and faa, the lord, and the great god, standing there. The Ashvins, the eight Vasus, and the great mighty Maruts, and the world gods and the Sādhya, were all present there. Kakotaka, AIA, Vāsuki, the serpent, Kacchapa, Apakunda, and Takṣaka, the great serpent. They were endowed with great energy, very angry, and very powerful. Many other serpents were also present there. Tārṣya, Ariṣṭanemi, Garuḍa with the black flag, and Aruṇa and Aruṇi, the sons of Vena, were present there. The great wonder astonished the best of the sages. The Pāṇḍavas were then engaged in their activities.

Page 481

Let there be your grace upon me, and may it also be beneficial for you. Indeed, I am like a pillar due to my attachment to Kunti. If you are pleased, then inspire her yourself. Pāṇḍu said: This is always changing for me, O Mādrī. I cannot speak to you with the intention of what is desirable or undesirable. Knowing your opinion, I will strive for the utmost. I believe that what I say will be fulfilled. Then Pāṇḍu again spoke to Kuntī in solitude: O Kuntī, please make my progeny dear to the world. And for the destruction of my enemies, and for my own sake, O auspicious one, do the utmost good. When you perform the difficult task, you will attain sovereignty through Indra's sacrifices, which are desired by the worthy. Thus, the wise brāhmaṇas, through austerity, approach the gurus for fame, O beautiful one. Thus, all the kings and the brāhmaṇas, the ascetics, performed the difficult task for fame. May I, like a boat, save Mādrī, the blameless one.

Page 482

By the division of offspring, may you attain the highest glory. Thus, having spoken to Mādrī, she thought of the deity. Therefore, your offspring will undoubtedly be suitable. Then, considering Mādrī, she went to the Ashvins in her mind. Upon their arrival, she gave birth to the twins. Ae, with Sahadeva, unparalleled in form on earth. Likewise, the twins also spoke with a body. Endowed with form, essence, and qualities, they shine with the wealth of beauty. They named them, the residents of the Jātash, and also with devotion, they honored the lord of the twenty-one. They called the eldest Yudhishthira, the middle one Bhīma, and the third Ajauṇa, thus imagining the sons of Kuntī. The former was Nakula, and thus Sahadeva, and the sons of Mādrī were spoken of by the pleased brāhmaṇas. Even after a year, you are born, O best of the Kurus. Then Kuntī again urged, "What is this?" The wise Pṛthā, always speaking the secret of kings, said to him. She said, "I am deceived by this, having once been told." I fear the fate of women. The birth is auspicious.

YOR possible winds With Palāśa, Tirakeśa, and Champaka, with Paribhadraka; And others with many trees, with the abundance of flowers. In the water place, adorned with various lotuses; Seeing the Pāṇḍava, Manmatha arose in the heart. There, delighting in the mind, like an immortal, He approached him, wearing a beautiful garment. Observing him, he, of that youthful form, His desire grew, like a rising sun in the deep. The king, with a secret heart, with lotus-like eyes, Could not control that desire, overpowered by the force of desire. Then the king forcibly seized her, who was hidden, With the goddess, shining forth as per her strength. He, with a heart filled with desire, did not understand that curse, Going towards Mādrī, in the act of love, like a force. At the end of life, the Kaurava, blessed by Manmatha, Leaving behind the fear of Rāpa, went away. His desire-filled mind was directly bewildered by time; Overcoming the group of senses, lost along with the mind. He, united with her, O Kurunandana, Pāṇḍu, with a supreme soul, engaged in the duty of time. Then Mādrī, embraced, approached the king, whose mind was gone.
